[Director 指令] [Patch — 正文压缩 2 页] 正文 10 页超限 2 页，按以下策略压缩：

## 策略 A: §6 Related Work 移至 Appendix（目标省 ~1 页）
- §6 正文只保留 1 段（~5-8 句），概述 OntoCoDec 在 landscape 中的定位，指向 Appendix 的 Extended Related Work
- 措辞参考："We discuss related work in detail in Appendix~\ref{app:related_work}; here we highlight key distinctions."
- 然后用 2-3 句话区分：(1) grammar-constrained decoding（syntactic only）(2) vocabulary/type-constrained generation（static sets）(3) post-hoc validation（detect-then-fix）vs OntoCoDec（semantic + dynamic + prevention）
- 已有 appendix_related_work.tex 是完整的 extended version，直接作为 appendix

## 策略 B: §5.1 Violation Landscape 压缩（目标省 ~0.5-1 页）
- Violation landscape 的详细数据表和分析移至 appendix
- §5 正文只保留关键结论（1 段）：baseline violation rates 的范围、κ 与 violation 的相关性、motivate 后续实验
- 引用 appendix 的详细表格

## 策略 C: 全文微调压缩
- 检查所有 section 是否有可以再压缩的段落（过度解释、重复论点）
- 特别是 §3 Method 和 §4 Composition 中，如果有冗余描述可以精简

**优先级**：A > B > C。A+B 应该能省 1.5-2 页，达到 8 页限制。如果 A+B 不够再做 C。

**硬约束**：
- 压缩不能丢失核心 claim 的支撑证据
- 所有移至 appendix 的内容必须在正文有引用
- 压缩后重新编译，确认正文 ≤ 8 页

修改后汇报页数和压缩细节。

5	XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar} (benchmarked in JSONSchemaBench~\cite{jsonschemabench2025}), Outlines~\cite{willard2023outlines}, LMQL~\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql}, DOMINO~\cite{beurer-kellner2024domino}, SynCode~\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and ASAp~\cite{park2024grammar} guarantee format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints such as domain/range rules.
6	
7	\paragraph{Type-Constrained Generation for IE and Code.}
8	Synchromesh~\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022}, PICARD~\cite{scholak2021picard}, and \citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} enforce type constraints in code generation; OntoCoDec migrates this to IE.
9	GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd}, GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie}, and GUIDEX~\cite{guidex2025} constrain \emph{which tokens} appear but not \emph{which type combinations} are valid per relation.
10	DORE~\cite{vukovic2024dore} uses generate-then-select with confidence thresholds; GenIE~\cite{josifoski2022genie} constrains entities via a trie without relation-specific type enforcement; EnriCo~\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} applies ASP constraints with task-specific manually authored rules.
11	BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025} combines constrained and unconstrained IE outputs via a trained boosting model; OntoCoDec is training-free and enforces per-instance type constraints rather than providing statistical improvement.
12	Training-based methods~\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie,schema_rl2025} provide only soft guidance; post-hoc approaches~\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025,slot2025} validate or restructure outputs after generation rather than preventing violations.
13	
14	\paragraph{Constrained Inference for IE.}
15	EnriCo~\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} formulates joint entity-relation extraction as a constrained optimization problem, using Answer Set Programming to enforce structural constraints (non-overlapping spans, valid type combinations) at inference time.
16	However, EnriCo operates on encoder-based classification models rather than autoregressive LLMs, requires supervised training for its representation module, and relies on hand-crafted ASP rules that must be manually derived from annotation guidelines for each dataset; on SciERC, no domain/range rules could be defined due to insufficient guideline detail.
17	OntoCoDec is training-free, applies to any autoregressive LLM, and compiles all constraints automatically from a declarative schema.
18	
19	\paragraph{General-Purpose Constrained Generation.}
20	IterGen~\cite{ugare2025itergen} provides grammar-guided generation with backtracking: users navigate forward and backward through generated output to programmatically enforce semantic constraints.
21	While effective for SQL and privacy protection, IterGen is a general-purpose library requiring manual Python implementation of each constraint, and its reactive generate-check-backtrack paradigm incurs 10--60\% overhead from discarded tokens.
22	OntoCoDec proactively masks invalid tokens during decoding, derives IE-specific constraints automatically from the ontology schema, and achieves $<$15\% overhead.
23	
24	\paragraph{Quality Impact.}
25	Recent work shows constrained decoding can degrade reasoning and distort output distributions~\cite{banerjee2025crane,grammar_aligned_2024,schall2025hiddencost,formattax2026}; DCCD~\cite{dccd2026} proposes unconstrained-then-constrained correction as an alternative.
26	However, extraction tasks are robust to constraints~\cite{tam2024letmespeak}: OntoCoDec's narrow type-slot constraints yield neutral F1 on SciERC and significant improvement on DocRED ($+$0.50\,pp, $p{<}0.001$); post-hoc filtering is impractical for EE (99.6\% argument loss; extended survey in Appendix~\ref{app:related_work}).
27	
```

1	% §5.1 Experimental Setup and Violation Landscape (compressed)
2	% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,
3	%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema
4	
5	\label{sec:violation_landscape}
6	
7	\paragraph{Setup.}
8	We evaluate on two IE benchmarks:
9	\textbf{SciERC}~\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, avg.\ 26.1 valid type pairs/relation) and \textbf{DocRED}~\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, avg.\ 5.68 valid pairs).
10	Primary model: Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \S\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \S\ref{sec:composability}.
11	We measure four violation categories: \textbf{DR} (domain/range type pair $\notin \sigma(r)$), \textbf{RV} (relation $\notin \mathcal{R}$), \textbf{ET} (entity type $\notin \mathcal{E}$), and \textbf{CF} (same entity assigned conflicting types across triples).
12	Violation rates are computed over successfully parsed documents; including failures as zero-violation instances adjusts rates by at most 0.23\,pp.
13	
14	\paragraph{Baseline violations and motivation.}
15	DR violations affect 15.37\% of triples on SciERC and 18.46\% on DocRED, comparable rates despite 14$\times$ ontology size difference; severity is driven by \emph{constraint tightness} rather than scale.
16	Per-relation rates vary by over 40\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}), correlating with tightness $\kappa(r)$.
17	RV on DocRED reaches 29.66\%: large vocabularies exceed prompt-based reproduction capacity.
18	Schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\% on SciERC but only 30\% on DocRED and is per-relation unreliable (DR for \textsc{Evaluate-for} \emph{increases} by 5.8\,pp). These limitations motivate decoding-time enforcement.
19	
```

```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex:5:XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar} (benchmarked in JSONSchemaBench~\cite{jsonschemabench2025}), Outlines~\cite{willard2023outlines}, LMQL~\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql}, DOMINO~\cite{beurer-kellner2024domino}, SynCode~\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and ASAp~\cite{park2024grammar} guarantee format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints such as domain/range rules.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex:9:GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd}, GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie}, and GUIDEX~\cite{guidex2025} constrain \emph{which tokens} appear but not \emph{which type combinations} are valid per relation.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex:10:DORE~\cite{vukovic2024dore} uses generate-then-select with confidence thresholds; GenIE~\cite{josifoski2022genie} constrains entities via a trie without relation-specific type enforcement; EnriCo~\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} applies ASP constraints with task-specific manually authored rules.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex:11:BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025} combines constrained and unconstrained IE outputs via a trained boosting model; OntoCoDec is training-free and enforces per-instance type constraints rather than providing statistical improvement.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex:12:Training-based methods~\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie,schema_rl2025} provide only soft guidance; post-hoc approaches~\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025,slot2025} validate or restructure outputs after generation rather than preventing violations.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex:20:IterGen~\cite{ugare2025itergen} provides grammar-guided generation with backtracking: users navigate forward and backward through generated output to programmatically enforce semantic constraints.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex:25:Recent work shows constrained decoding can degrade reasoning and distort output distributions~\cite{banerjee2025crane,grammar_aligned_2024,schall2025hiddencost,formattax2026}; DCCD~\cite{dccd2026} proposes unconstrained-then-constrained correction as an alternative.
```

    "snippet": "5\t%\n6\tWithout constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations: 15.37\\% on SciERC (7 relations) and 18.46\\% on DocRED (96 relations), with 1--2\\% persisting even after fine-tuning; rates are driven by \\emph{constraint tightness} rather than ontology or model scale.\n7\tComposed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning, \\textsc{OntoCoDec} reduces DR to $\\leq$\\,0.20\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp of unconstrained baselines (R-F1 40--46): near-lossless composability at deployment-grade extraction quality.\n8\t% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\n9\tUnder zero-shot decoding, which stress-tests constraint robustness at low extraction quality (R-F1 2--8\\%), DR drops by 42--99\\% across three model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) with no significant F1 cost (DocRED: $+$0.50\\,pp, $p{<}0.001$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n10\tWith beam search ($k{=}4$), the full stack eliminates all four violation types under fine-tuned beam decoding (SciERC, both Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B) while preserving extraction quality (R-F1 44.23 and 46.11, respectively); in zero-shot beam decoding, DR reaches 0\\% for calibrated models on both RE datasets.\n11\tThe framework generalizes to event extraction (WikiEvents: 56 event types, 80 roles) without architectural modification and eliminates event-vocabulary and role-vocabulary violations ($\\to$ 0\\%); where unconstrained outputs are severely off-schema, format compliance additionally enables entity extraction (Entity F1: 0\\%\\,$\\to$\\,12--18\\%).\n12\tFine-tuning composability extends to EE: LoRA + S1 eliminates RoV and reduces AC to $\\leq$\\,0.8\\% on WikiEvents.\n13\tPer-slot enforcement introduces a cross-field consistency trade-off (up to 12\\% on RE and 21\\% on EE in zero-shot, resolved to $\\leq$1.2\\% by fine-tuning on RE).\n14\tOntoCoDec demonstrates that ontology schemas can be compiled into decoding-time constraints that compose losslessly with standard fine-tuning and generalize across four models spanning three tokenizer families and two model scales (8B to 70B). The framework bridges the gap between flexible generative extraction and the type-safety requirements of downstream knowledge systems.\n15\t\\end{abstract}"
  },

1	% Section 2: Background (compressed)
2	
3	\section{Background}
4	\label{sec:background}
5	
6	\paragraph{IE Ontology Structure.}
7	\label{sec:bg:ontology}
8	An IE ontology $\mathcal{O} = (\mathcal{E}, \mathcal{R}, \sigma)$ defines entity types $\mathcal{E}$, relation types $\mathcal{R}$, and a mapping $\sigma: \mathcal{R} \to \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{E} \times \mathcal{E})$ specifying valid (head type, tail type) pairs per relation.
9	A triple $(h, r, t)$ is \emph{type-valid} if $(\mathrm{type}(h), \mathrm{type}(t)) \in \sigma(r)$.
10	We define the \emph{constraint tightness} of $r$ as $1 - |\sigma(r)| / |\mathcal{E}|^2$: tighter constraints leave fewer valid options, increasing violation likelihood.
11	
12	\paragraph{Grammar-Constrained Decoding.}
13	\label{sec:bg:gcd}
14	Grammar-constrained decoding restricts each token $y_t$ to a mask $M_g \in \{0, 1\}^{|V|}$ derived from a target grammar.
15	XGrammar~\citep{dong2025xgrammar} compiles JSON Schemas into pushdown automata for efficient enforcement of structural constraints; Outlines~\citep{willard2023outlines} and SGLang~\citep{zheng2024sglang} offer similar capabilities.
16	However, these methods are limited to context-free constraints and cannot enforce cross-field semantic rules (e.g., ``if $r$ = \textsc{Feature-of}, then $\mathrm{type}(h) \in \{\textsc{Method}, \textsc{OtherScientificTerm}\}$''), which depend on previously generated field values.
17	
18	\paragraph{Problem Statement.}
19	\label{sec:bg:problem}
20	Given $\mathcal{O}$ and an autoregressive LM $\mathcal{M}$, we seek constraints $\mathcal{C}$ ensuring: (a)~\textbf{structural validity}, i.e., well-formed JSON with $\mathcal{E}$/$\mathcal{R}$ membership; and (b)~\textbf{type validity}, i.e., $(\mathrm{type}(h), \mathrm{type}(t)) \in \sigma(r)$ for every triple.
21	Condition (a) is context-free; (b) is context-sensitive but \emph{prefix-decidable}: at the point where a type token is generated, all information needed (relation type, entity registry) is available in the decoded prefix.
22	This requires that each entity type name maps to a unique first subword token (\S\ref{sec:semantic}, Implementation), a design assumption verified for all tested configurations.
23	
```

```text
1	% Section 3 — Method (Draft)
2	% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction
3	% via Compiled Ontology Constraints
4	
5	\section{Method}
6	\label{sec:method}
7	
8	We present OntoCoDec, a two-layer constrained decoding framework that compiles ontology schemas into token-level masks enforced during autoregressive generation.
9	Unlike type-constrained code generation, IE presents three distinct challenges: (1)~entity types are inferred from free text rather than declared annotations, (2)~the entity registry is built dynamically during generation, and (3)~constraint targets are context-dependent type assignments rather than fixed grammar productions.
10	The grammar layer (\S\ref{sec:grammar}) guarantees structural validity (well-formed JSON with ontology-vocabulary membership), while the semantic layer (\S\ref{sec:semantic}) enforces domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry.
11	We describe the problem formulation (\S\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\S\ref{sec:strategies}).
12	Formal analysis of constraint composition is deferred to Section~\ref{sec:composition}.
13	
14	
15	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
16	\label{sec:formulation}
17	
18	\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}
19	Given an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:
20	%
21	\begin{equation}\label{eq:output-schema}
22	\resizebox{.88\columnwidth}{!}{$\displaystyle
23	  y = \bigl\{\texttt{entities}: [\{n_i, \tau_i\}],\; \texttt{relations}: [\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \tau^h_j, \tau^t_j\}]\bigr\}
24	$}
25	\end{equation}
26	%
27	where each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \in \mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\tau^h_j, \tau^t_j$.
28	The output JSON Schema (\S\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\to$ tail $\to$ relation $\to$ head\_type $\to$ tail\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation.
29	This ordering ensures that the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields are generated, which enables domain and range lookups in sequential masking (\S\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).
30	
31	\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}
32	Using the ontology $\mathcal{O} = (\mathcal{E}, \mathcal{R}, \sigma)$ defined in \S\ref{sec:background}, we write $\operatorname{dom}(r) = \{\tau^h : \exists \tau^t,\, (\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h) = \{\tau^t : (\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\tau^h$.
33	Our enforcement target is \emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)$ for every triple.
34	The decoder enforces this sequentially: $\tau^h \in \operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\tau^t \in \operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h)$ at tail position. This is equivalent to the full pair check and strictly stronger than independent domain/range enforcement, because most relations have non-rectangular type signatures ($\sigma(r) \subsetneq \operatorname{dom}(r) \times \operatorname{range}(r)$ for all 7 SciERC and 73/96 DocRED relations; see \S\ref{sec:rectangularity}).\footnote{An independent check ($\tau^h \in \operatorname{dom}(r) \wedge \tau^t \in \operatorname{range}(r)$) would over-permit type pairs by $1.50\times$ on SciERC and $1.58\times$ on DocRED, corresponding to 33.3\% and 36.8\% spurious type pairs.}
35	Our goal is maximizing $P(y \mid x)$ subject to all triples being type-valid and $y$ being well-formed JSON conforming to Eq.~\eqref{eq:output-schema}.
36	
37	
38	\subsection{Grammar Layer}
39	\label{sec:grammar}
40	
41	The grammar layer compiles $\mathcal{O}$ into a context-free grammar (CFG) enforced at every decoding step via logit masking.
42	This compilation proceeds in two stages.
43	
44	\paragraph{Schema-to-grammar compilation.}
45	We generate a JSON Schema encoding the structure in Eq.~\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \texttt{enum} constraints restricting entity types to $\mathcal{E}$ and relation types to $\mathcal{R}$.
46	This schema is compiled into an EBNF grammar via XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, producing a pushdown automaton tracking JSON structure and enum membership.
47	
48	\paragraph{Logit masking.}
49	At step $t$, the automaton computes grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \subseteq V$; all others receive logits $-\infty$.
50	This simultaneously enforces: (i)~structural validity (well-formed JSON), (ii)~relation vocabulary membership ($r_j \in \mathcal{R}$), and (iii)~entity type membership ($\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$).
51	Domain/range constraints are \emph{not} enforced, as they depend on entity types within each triple; this gap is filled by the semantic layer.
52	
53	\paragraph{Empirical profile.}
54	On SciERC, grammar constraints eliminate relation vocabulary violations entirely (1.39\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and entity type violations entirely (0.40\%\,$\to$\,0\%), with no measurable F1 impact.
55	Domain/range violations remain effectively unchanged (15.37\%\,$\to$\,14.84\%). This confirms that grammar constraints enforce structural but not semantic validity.
56	
57	
58	\subsection{Semantic Layer}
59	\label{sec:semantic}
60	
61	The semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.
62	
63	\subsubsection{Entity Registry}
64	\label{sec:entity-registry}
65	
66	As the model generates the entity list, the semantic layer builds a registry $\mathcal{D}: \text{name} \to \text{type}$ mapping each entity to its declared type.
67	This registry is populated incrementally: when the model completes an entry $\{n_i, \tau_i\}$, the pair $(n_i, \tau_i)$ is added to $\mathcal{D}$.
68	The registry is then consulted during relation generation to look up the declared types of head and tail entities.
69	
70	\subsubsection{Position Detection}
71	\label{sec:position-detection}
72	
73	At each step $t$, the processor determines which field is currently being generated by pattern-matching on the decoded prefix.
74	We define five field positions (\textsc{EntityType}, \textsc{RelationType}, \textsc{HeadType}, \textsc{TailType}, and \textsc{FreeText}), identified via JSON key patterns (e.g., tokens following \texttt{"head\_type":} indicate \textsc{HeadType}).
75	Semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\textsc{HeadType}, \textsc{TailType}).
76	
77	\subsubsection{Dynamic Masking}
78	\label{sec:dynamic-masking}
79	
80	At type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \subseteq V$ restricting generation to type-valid tokens.
81	
82	\paragraph{Head type masking.}
83	At a \textsc{HeadType} position, the processor considers the entity registry $\mathcal{D}$ and the current relation type $r_j$ (if already generated).
84	If $h_j \in \mathcal{D}$ and $\mathcal{D}[h_j] \in \operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only that registered type: $S(t) = \{\operatorname{tok}(\mathcal{D}[h_j])\}$, ensuring consistency with both the entity list and domain constraints.
85	Otherwise, if $r_j$ is known, the mask falls back to all domain-valid types: $S(t) = \{\operatorname{tok}(\tau) : \tau \in \operatorname{dom}(r_j)\}$.
86	If $r_j$ is unavailable, no semantic mask is applied.
87	
88	\paragraph{Tail type masking.}
89	At a \textsc{TailType} position, the head type $\tau^h_j$ is already determined from the decoded prefix.
90	The processor computes $\operatorname{range}(r_j, \tau^h_j)$, the valid tail types conditioned on the generated head type (\S\ref{sec:formulation}).
91	If $t_j \in \mathcal{D}$, the mask further restricts to the registered type when it belongs to this set.
92	This sequential check ($\tau^h \in \operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\tau^t \in \operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h)$) achieves exact pair-based enforcement, strictly tighter than an independent range check $\tau^t \in \operatorname{range}(r)$.
93	
94	\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}
95	Each type name $\tau \in \mathcal{E}$ must be mapped to token IDs for logit masking.
96	A naive approach maps $\tau$ to its first subword token and sets disallowed IDs to $-\infty$, but this fails in two scenarios:
97	(1)~SentencePiece tokenizers produce context-dependent tokenizations where the same string yields different token IDs depending on preceding context (e.g., after a JSON delimiter), and
98	(2)~ontologies with prefix-shared type names (e.g., \textsc{Sen}, \textsc{Sid}, \textsc{Side} in WikiEvents) map to the same first token, making single-token disambiguation impossible.
99	We resolve both with \emph{continuation masking}: at compile time, we scan the full vocabulary to build a prefix map identifying, for each type name, all tokens whose decoded text is a valid prefix.
100	At decode time, the semantic mask allows any token that continues a partially-generated type name, disambiguating over multiple steps.
101	This is tokenizer-agnostic, handling byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral) without tokenizer-specific logic.
102	
103	\subsubsection{Grammar-Semantic Interaction}
104	\label{sec:interaction}
105	
106	The final mask is the conjunction of $G(t)$ and $S(t)$, permitting only tokens satisfying both layers (formalized in \S\ref{sec:composition}).
107	This embodies an \emph{enabling dependency}: semantic lookup requires relation types to be valid members of $\mathcal{R}$, guaranteed only by the grammar layer.
108	Without grammar enforcement, the model may generate out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no $\sigma(r)$ rules exist.
109	Empirically, semantic-only constraints reduce DR by only 24\% (15.37\%\,$\to$\,11.65\%) while failing to eliminate vocabulary violations; the full combination achieves 94\% DR reduction.
110	When $S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$ (the semantic layer would mask out all grammar-valid tokens), the system falls back to grammar-only control, preserving structural validity at the cost of a potential type violation.
111	This \emph{grammar-first invariant} reflects a deliberate priority ordering: structural integrity is a hard constraint, while type violations are soft errors detectable by downstream validation.
112	
113	
114	\subsection{Decoding Strategies}
115	\label{sec:strategies}
116	
117	\paragraph{S1: Single-pass decoding.}
118	Both constraint layers are active throughout a single generation pass.
119	The entity registry is populated as entities are generated and immediately available for relation-level type checking.
120	This is the simpler and recommended strategy. It achieves strong constraint enforcement with higher relation F1 than the two-pass alternative.
121	
122	\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\label{sec:s2}
123	The first pass generates only entities under grammar constraints; the extracted entities compile an input-dependent grammar that additionally constrains head and tail names to match declared entities.
124	The second pass generates relations under this tighter grammar plus semantic constraints.
125	Detailed comparison is provided in the appendix.
126	
```

constraints reduce DR by only 24\\% (15.37\\%\\,$\\to$\\,11.65\\%) while failing to eliminate vocabulary violations; the full combination achieves 94\\% DR reduction.\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$ (the semantic layer would mask out all grammar-valid tokens), the system falls back to grammar-only control, preserving structural validity at the cost of a potential type violation.\nThis \\emph{grammar-first invariant} reflects a deliberate priority ordering: structural integrity is a hard constraint, while type violations are soft errors detectable by downstream validation.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1: Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth constraint layers are active throughout a single generation pass.\nThe entity registry is populated as entities are generated and immediately available for relation-level type checking.\nThis is the simpler and recommended strategy. It achieves strong constraint enforcement with higher relation F1 than the two-pass alternative.\n\n\\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nThe first pass generates only entities under grammar constraints; the extracted entities compile an input-dependent grammar that additionally constrains head and tail names to match declared entities.\nThe second pass generates relations under this tighter grammar plus semantic constraints.\nDetailed comparison is provided in the appendix.\n",

    }

  "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nWe provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec.\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql,ugare2024syncode,park2024grammar,beurer-kellner2024domino,jsonschemabench2025} guarantees format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints; type-constrained code generation~\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022,scholak2021picard,pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compiles type systems into token-level masks but operates on static type declarations, whereas IE requires a dynamic entity registry built during generation.\nVocabulary-constrained IE methods~\\cite{geng2023gcd,sainz2024gollie,josifoski2022genie,guidex2025,vukovic2024dore,boostcd2025} restrict which tokens can appear but not which type combinations are valid per relation; EnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} enforces structural constraints via ASP but requires supervised training and hand-crafted rules; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} supports semantic constraints via backtracking but requires manual implementation per constraint.\nTraining-based methods~\\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie,schema_rl2025} provide soft guidance; post-hoc approaches~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025,slot2025} validate outputs after generation.\nOntoCoDec prevents violations during decoding, is training-free, and compiles constraints automatically from ontology schemas.\nRecent work shows constrained decoding can degrade reasoning~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,grammar_aligned_2024,schall2025hiddencost,formattax2026,dccd2026}; however, extraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak}, and OntoCoDec constrains only type tokens while leaving entity spans unconstrained.\n"
}

    "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nWe provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec.\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql,ugare2024syncode,park2024grammar,beurer-kellner2024domino,jsonschemabench2025} guarantees format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints; type-constrained code generation~\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022,scholak2021picard,pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compiles type systems into token-level masks but operates on static type declarations, whereas IE requires a dynamic entity registry built during generation.\nVocabulary-constrained IE methods~\\cite{geng2023gcd,sainz2024gollie,josifoski2022genie,guidex2025,vukovic2024dore,boostcd2025} restrict which tokens can appear but not which type combinations are valid per relation; EnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} enforces structural constraints via ASP but requires supervised training and hand-crafted rules; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} supports semantic constraints via backtracking but requires manual implementation per constraint.\nTraining-based methods~\\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie,schema_rl2025} provide soft guidance; post-hoc approaches~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025,slot2025} validate outputs after generation.\nOntoCoDec prevents violations during decoding, is training-free, and compiles constraints automatically from ontology schemas.\nRecent work shows constrained decoding can degrade reasoning~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,grammar_aligned_2024,schall2025hiddencost,formattax2026,dccd2026}; however, extraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak}, and OntoCoDec constrains only type tokens while leaving entity spans unconstrained.\n",

      {

          " \\section{Related Work}",
          " \\label{sec:related_work}",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Grammar-Constrained Decoding.}",
          "-XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar} (benchmarked in JSONSchemaBench~\\cite{jsonschemabench2025}), Outlines~\\cite{willard2023outlines}, LMQL~\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql}, DOMINO~\\cite{beurer-kellner2024domino}, SynCode~\\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and ASAp~\\cite{park2024grammar} guarantee format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints such as domain/range rules.",

          "-\\paragraph{Type-Constrained Generation for IE and Code.}",
          "-Synchromesh~\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022}, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard}, and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} enforce type constraints in code generation; OntoCoDec migrates this to IE.",

          "-Training-based methods~\\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie,schema_rl2025} provide only soft guidance; post-hoc approaches~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025,slot2025} validate or restructure outputs after generation rather than preventing violations.",

          "-EnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} formulates joint entity-relation extraction as a constrained optimization problem, using Answer Set Programming to enforce structural constraints (non-overlapping spans, valid type combinations) at inference time.",
          "-However, EnriCo operates on encoder-based classification models rather than autoregressive LLMs, requires supervised training for its representation module, and relies on hand-crafted ASP rules that must be manually derived from annotation guidelines for each dataset; on SciERC, no domain/range rules could be defined due to insufficient guideline detail.",

          "-",

          "-IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} provides grammar-guided generation with backtracking: users navigate forward and backward through generated output to programmatically enforce semantic constraints.",

          "-Recent work shows constrained decoding can degrade reasoning and distort output distributions~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,grammar_aligned_2024,schall2025hiddencost,formattax2026}; DCCD~\\cite{dccd2026} proposes unconstrained-then-constrained correction as an alternative.",
          "-However, extraction tasks are robust to constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak}: OntoCoDec's narrow type-slot constraints yield neutral F1 on SciERC and significant improvement on DocRED ($+$0.50\\,pp, $p{<}0.001$); post-hoc filtering is impractical for EE (99.6\\% argument loss; extended survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}).",
          "+We provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec.",
          "+Grammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql,ugare2024syncode,park2024grammar,beurer-kellner2024domino,jsonschemabench2025} guarantees format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints; type-constrained code generation~\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022,scholak2021picard,pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compiles type systems into token-level masks but operates on static type declarations, whereas IE requires a dynamic entity registry built during generation.",
          "+Vocabulary-constrained IE methods~\\cite{geng2023gcd,sainz2024gollie,josifoski2022genie,guidex2025,vukovic2024dore,boostcd2025} restrict which tokens can appear but not which type combinations are valid per relation; EnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} enforces structural constraints via ASP but requires supervised training and hand-crafted rules; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} supports semantic constraints via backtracking but requires manual implementation per constraint.",
          "+Training-based methods~\\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie,schema_rl2025} provide soft guidance; post-hoc approaches~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025,slot2025} validate outputs after generation.",
          "+OntoCoDec prevents violations during decoding, is training-free, and compiles constraints automatically from ontology schemas.",
          "+Recent work shows constrained decoding can degrade reasoning~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,grammar_aligned_2024,schall2025hiddencost,formattax2026,dccd2026}; however, extraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak}, and OntoCoDec constrains only type tokens while leaving entity spans unconstrained."
        ]
      }
    ],
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-Constrained Decoding.}\nXGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar} (benchmarked in JSONSchemaBench~\\cite{jsonschemabench2025}), Outlines~\\cite{willard2023outlines}, LMQL~\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql}, DOMINO~\\cite{beurer-kellner2024domino}, SynCode~\\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and ASAp~\\cite{park2024grammar} guarantee format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints such as domain/range rules.\n\n\\paragraph{Type-Constrained Generation for IE and Code.}\nSynchromesh~\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022}, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard}, and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} enforce type constraints in code generation; OntoCoDec migrates this to IE.\nGCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie}, and GUIDEX~\\cite{guidex2025} constrain \\emph{which tokens} appear but not \\emph{which type combinations} are valid per relation.\nDORE~\\cite{vukovic2024dore} uses generate-then-select with confidence thresholds; GenIE~\\cite{josifoski2022genie} constrains entities via a trie without relation-specific type enforcement; EnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} applies ASP constraints with task-specific manually authored rules.\nBoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025} combines constrained and unconstrained IE outputs via a trained boosting model; OntoCoDec is training-free and enforces per-instance type constraints rather than providing statistical improvement.\nTraining-based methods~\\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie,schema_rl2025} provide only soft guidance; post-hoc approaches~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025,slot2025} validate or restructure outputs after generation rather than preventing violations.\n\n\\paragraph{Constrained Inference for IE.}\nEnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} formulates joint entity-relation extraction as a constrained optimization problem, using Answer Set Programming to enforce structural constraints (non-overlapping spans, valid type combinations) at inference time.\nHowever, EnriCo operates on encoder-based classification models rather than autoregressive LLMs, requires supervised training for its representation module, and relies on hand-crafted ASP rules that must be manually derived from annotation guidelines for each dataset; on SciERC, no domain/range rules could be defined due to insufficient guideline detail.\nOntoCoDec is training-free, applies to any autoregressive LLM, and compiles all constraints automatically from a declarative schema.\n\n\\paragraph{General-Purpose Constrained Generation.}\nIterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} provides grammar-guided generation with backtracking: users navigate forward and backward through generated output to programmatically enforce semantic constraints.\nWhile effective for SQL and privacy protection, IterGen is a general-purpose library requiring manual Python implementation of each constraint, and its reactive generate-check-backtrack paradigm incurs 10--60\\% overhead from discarded tokens.\nOntoCoDec proactively masks invalid tokens during decoding, derives IE-specific constraints automatically from the ontology schema, and achieves $<$15\\% overhead.\n\n\\paragraph{Quality Impact.}\nRecent work shows constrained decoding can degrade reasoning and distort output distributions~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,grammar_aligned_2024,schall2025hiddencost,formattax2026}; DCCD~\\cite{dccd2026} proposes unconstrained-then-constrained correction as an alternative.\nHowever, extraction tasks are robust to constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak}: OntoCoDec's narrow type-slot constraints yield neutral F1 on SciERC and significant improvement on DocRED ($+$0.50\\,pp, $p{<}0.001$); post-hoc filtering is impractical for EE (99.6\\% argument loss; extended survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}).\n",
    "userModified": false
  },

for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.27\\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.\nCF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\\,pp). CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).\nEntity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\\,pp for Qwen vs.\\ $-$0.64\\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\\% vs.\\ 6.8\\%).\nZero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\\% compared to $\\sim$45\\% for fine-tuned systems.\nComposability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp; EE: RoV$\\to$0\\%, AC$\\leq$0.8\\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\\%, Llama 0.46\\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.\nThe continuation masking approach (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}) handles multi-token type names and prefix-shared types on the ontologies tested (6--27 entity types); scalability to ontologies with hundreds of entity types has not been validated, and the prefix-map size may grow substantially.\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated only on 8B-class models.\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern's predictive scope may differ at other scales.\nCF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.\nResidual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\nSchema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) are irreducible by decoding-time constraints alone (training-set frequency coverage); complementary approaches such as global type consensus or post-hoc correction may address this limitation.\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC test set (551 documents) and a DocRED subset across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.\nEvaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA) on two 8B-class models; scaling fine-tuning to 70B-class models, other training paradigms (full fine-tuning, RLHF), and non-English ontologies remains to be explored.\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

    "content": "% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)\n% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,\n%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema\n\n\\label{sec:violation_landscape}\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.}\nWe evaluate on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$).\nPrimary model: Qwen3-8B~\\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \\S\\ref{sec:composability}.\nWe measure four violation categories: \\textbf{DR} (domain/range type pair $\\notin \\sigma(r)$), \\textbf{RV} (relation $\\notin \\mathcal{R}$), \\textbf{ET} (entity type $\\notin \\mathcal{E}$), and \\textbf{CF} (same entity assigned conflicting types across triples).\nBaseline DR violations are 15.37\\% (SciERC) and 18.46\\% (DocRED), comparable rates driven by constraint tightness rather than ontology scale (\\S\\ref{sec:introduction}); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\\% on SciERC but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\n",

          "+We evaluate on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$).",
          " Primary model: Qwen3-8B~\\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \\S\\ref{sec:composability}.",
          " We measure four violation categories: \\textbf{DR} (domain/range type pair $\\notin \\sigma(r)$), \\textbf{RV} (relation $\\notin \\mathcal{R}$), \\textbf{ET} (entity type $\\notin \\mathcal{E}$), and \\textbf{CF} (same entity assigned conflicting types across triples).",

    "originalFile": "% §5.1 Experimental Setup and Violation Landscape (compressed)\n% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,\n%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema\n\n\\label{sec:violation_landscape}\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.}\nWe evaluate on two IE benchmarks:\n\\textbf{SciERC}~\\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, avg.\\ 26.1 valid type pairs/relation) and \\textbf{DocRED}~\\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, avg.\\ 5.68 valid pairs).\nPrimary model: Qwen3-8B~\\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \\S\\ref{sec:composability}.\nWe measure four violation categories: \\textbf{DR} (domain/range type pair $\\notin \\sigma(r)$), \\textbf{RV} (relation $\\notin \\mathcal{R}$), \\textbf{ET} (entity type $\\notin \\mathcal{E}$), and \\textbf{CF} (same entity assigned conflicting types across triples).\nViolation rates are computed over successfully parsed documents; including failures as zero-violation instances adjusts rates by at most 0.23\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Baseline violations and motivation.}\nDR violations affect 15.37\\% of triples on SciERC and 18.46\\% on DocRED, comparable rates despite 14$\\times$ ontology size difference; severity is driven by \\emph{constraint tightness} rather than scale.\nPer-relation rates vary by over 40\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}), correlating with tightness $\\kappa(r)$.\nRV on DocRED reaches 29.66\\%: large vocabularies exceed prompt-based reproduction capacity.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\\% on SciERC but only 30\\% on DocRED and is per-relation unreliable (DR for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} \\emph{increases} by 5.8\\,pp). These limitations motivate decoding-time enforcement.\n",
    "userModified": false
  },

  "old_string": "\\subsubsection{Grammar-Semantic Interaction}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\n\nThe final mask is the conjunction of $G(t)$ and $S(t)$, permitting only tokens satisfying both layers (formalized in \\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\nThis embodies an \\emph{enabling dependency}: semantic lookup requires relation types to be valid members of $\\mathcal{R}$, guaranteed only by the grammar layer.\nWithout grammar enforcement, the model may generate out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no $\\sigma(r)$ rules exist.\nEmpirically, semantic-only constraints reduce DR by only 24\\% (15.37\\%\\,$\\to$\\,11.65\\%) while failing to eliminate vocabulary violations; the full combination achieves 94\\% DR reduction.\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$ (the semantic layer would mask out all grammar-valid tokens), the system falls back to grammar-only control, preserving structural validity at the cost of a potential type violation.\nThis \\emph{grammar-first invariant} reflects a deliberate priority ordering: structural integrity is a hard constraint, while type violations are soft errors detectable by downstream validation.",

    "newString": "\\subsubsection{Grammar-Semantic Interaction}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\n\nThe final mask is the conjunction of $G(t)$ and $S(t)$, permitting only tokens satisfying both layers.\nThis embodies an \\emph{enabling dependency}: semantic lookup requires relation types to be valid members of $\\mathcal{R}$, guaranteed only by the grammar layer; without it, semantic-only constraints reduce DR by only 24\\%.\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$, the system falls back to grammar-only control (\\emph{grammar-first fallback}), preserving structural validity at the cost of a potential type violation.\nFormal analysis of composition, fallback behavior, and constraint tightness is in \\S\\ref{sec:composition}.",
    "originalFile": "% Section 3 — Method (Draft)\n% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction\n% via Compiled Ontology Constraints\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe present OntoCoDec, a two-layer constrained decoding framework that compiles ontology schemas into token-level masks enforced during autoregressive generation.\nUnlike type-constrained code generation, IE presents three distinct challenges: (1)~entity types are inferred from free text rather than declared annotations, (2)~the entity registry is built dynamically during generation, and (3)~constraint targets are context-dependent type assignments rather than fixed grammar productions.\nThe grammar layer (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) guarantees structural validity (well-formed JSON with ontology-vocabulary membership), while the semantic layer (\\S\\ref{sec:semantic}) enforces domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry.\nWe describe the problem formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}).\nFormal analysis of constraint composition is deferred to Section~\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\n\\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}\nGiven an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:output-schema}\n\\resizebox{.88\\columnwidth}{!}{$\\displaystyle\n  y = \\bigl\\{\\texttt{entities}: [\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}],\\; \\texttt{relations}: [\\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j\\}]\\bigr\\}\n$}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j$.\nThe output JSON Schema (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\\to$ tail $\\to$ relation $\\to$ head\\_type $\\to$ tail\\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation.\nThis ordering ensures that the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields are generated, which enables domain and range lookups in sequential masking (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).\n\n\\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}\nUsing the ontology $\\mathcal{O} = (\\mathcal{E}, \\mathcal{R}, \\sigma)$ defined in \\S\\ref{sec:background}, we write $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau^h : \\exists \\tau^t,\\, (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h) = \\{\\tau^t : (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\\tau^h$.\nOur enforcement target is \\emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)$ for every triple.\nThe decoder enforces this sequentially: $\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$ at tail position. This is equivalent to the full pair check and strictly stronger than independent domain/range enforcement, because most relations have non-rectangular type signatures ($\\sigma(r) \\subsetneq \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\times \\operatorname{range}(r)$ for all 7 SciERC and 73/96 DocRED relations; see \\S\\ref{sec:rectangularity}).\\footnote{An independent check ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\wedge \\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r)$) would over-permit type pairs by $1.50\\times$ on SciERC and $1.58\\times$ on DocRED, corresponding to 33.3\\% and 36.8\\% spurious type pairs.}\nOur goal is maximizing $P(y \\mid x)$ subject to all triples being type-valid and $y$ being well-formed JSON conforming to Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Grammar Layer}\n\\label{sec:grammar}\n\nThe grammar layer compiles $\\mathcal{O}$ into a context-free grammar (CFG) enforced at every decoding step via logit masking.\nThis compilation proceeds in two stages.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-to-grammar compilation.}\nWe generate a JSON Schema encoding the structure in Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \\texttt{enum} constraints restricting entity types to $\\mathcal{E}$ and relation types to $\\mathcal{R}$.\nThis schema is compiled into an EBNF grammar via XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, producing a pushdown automaton tracking JSON structure and enum membership.\n\n\\paragraph{Logit masking.}\nAt step $t$, the automaton computes grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \\subseteq V$; all others receive logits $-\\infty$.\nThis simultaneously enforces: (i)~structural validity (well-formed JSON), (ii)~relation vocabulary membership ($r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$), and (iii)~entity type membership ($\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$).\nDomain/range constraints are \\emph{not} enforced, as they depend on entity types within each triple; this gap is filled by the semantic layer.\n\n\\subsection{Semantic Layer}\n\\label{sec:semantic}\n\nThe semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.\n\n\\subsubsection{Entity Registry}\n\\label{sec:entity-registry}\n\nAs the model generates the entity list, the semantic layer builds a registry $\\mathcal{D}: \\text{name} \\to \\text{type}$ mapping each entity to its declared type.\nThis registry is populated incrementally: when the model completes an entry $\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}$, the pair $(n_i, \\tau_i)$ is added to $\\mathcal{D}$.\nThe registry is then consulted during relation generation to look up the declared types of head and tail entities.\n\n\\subsubsection{Position Detection}\n\\label{sec:position-detection}\n\nAt each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\\textsc{HeadType}, \\textsc{TailType}).\n\n\\subsubsection{Dynamic Masking}\n\\label{sec:dynamic-masking}\n\nAt type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \\subseteq V$ restricting generation to type-valid tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{Head type masking.}\nAt a \\textsc{HeadType} position, the processor considers the entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ and the current relation type $r_j$ (if already generated).\nIf $h_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$ and $\\mathcal{D}[h_j] \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only that registered type: $S(t) = \\{\\operatorname{tok}(\\mathcal{D}[h_j])\\}$, ensuring consistency with both the entity list and domain constraints.\nOtherwise, if $r_j$ is known, the mask falls back to all domain-valid types: $S(t) = \\{\\operatorname{tok}(\\tau) : \\tau \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)\\}$.\nIf $r_j$ is unavailable, no semantic mask is applied.\n\n\\paragraph{Tail type masking.}\nAt a \\textsc{TailType} position, the head type $\\tau^h_j$ is already determined from the decoded prefix.\nThe processor computes $\\operatorname{range}(r_j, \\tau^h_j)$, the valid tail types conditioned on the generated head type (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}).\nIf $t_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$, the mask further restricts to the registered type when it belongs to this set.\nThis sequential check ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$) achieves exact pair-based enforcement, strictly tighter than an independent range check $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nEach type name $\\tau \\in \\mathcal{E}$ must be mapped to token IDs for logit masking.\nA naive approach maps $\\tau$ to its first subword token and sets disallowed IDs to $-\\infty$, but this fails in two scenarios:\n(1)~SentencePiece tokenizers produce context-dependent tokenizations where the same string yields different token IDs depending on preceding context (e.g., after a JSON delimiter), and\n(2)~ontologies with prefix-shared type names (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}, \\textsc{Sid}, \\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents) map to the same first token, making single-token disambiguation impossible.\nWe resolve both with \\emph{continuation masking}: at compile time, we scan the full vocabulary to build a prefix map identifying, for each type name, all tokens whose decoded text is a valid prefix.\nAt decode time, the semantic mask allows any token that continues a partially-generated type name, disambiguating over multiple steps.\nThis is tokenizer-agnostic, handling byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral) without tokenizer-specific logic.\n\n\\subsubsection{Grammar-Semantic Interaction}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\n\nThe final mask is the conjunction of $G(t)$ and $S(t)$, permitting only tokens satisfying both layers (formalized in \\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\nThis embodies an \\emph{enabling dependency}: semantic lookup requires relation types to be valid members of $\\mathcal{R}$, guaranteed only by the grammar layer.\nWithout grammar enforcement, the model may generate out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no $\\sigma(r)$ rules exist.\nEmpirically, semantic-only constraints reduce DR by only 24\\% (15.37\\%\\,$\\to$\\,11.65\\%) while failing to eliminate vocabulary violations; the full combination achieves 94\\% DR reduction.\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$ (the semantic layer would mask out all grammar-valid tokens), the system falls back to grammar-only control, preserving structural validity at the cost of a potential type violation.\nThis \\emph{grammar-first invariant} reflects a deliberate priority ordering: structural integrity is a hard constraint, while type violations are soft errors detectable by downstream validation.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1: Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth constraint layers are active throughout a single generation pass.\nThe entity registry is populated as entities are generated and immediately available for relation-level type checking.\nThis is the simpler and recommended strategy. It achieves strong constraint enforcement with higher relation F1 than the two-pass alternative.\n\n\\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nThe first pass generates only entities under grammar constraints; the extracted entities compile an input-dependent grammar that additionally constrains head and tail names to match declared entities.\nThe second pass generates relations under this tighter grammar plus semantic constraints.\nDetailed comparison is provided in the appendix.\n",

    "newString": "\\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nA first pass extracts entities under grammar constraints; extracted entities compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass, which generates relations under both grammar and semantic constraints.\nS1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.",
    "originalFile": "% Section 3 — Method (Draft)\n% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction\n% via Compiled Ontology Constraints\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe present OntoCoDec, a two-layer constrained decoding framework that compiles ontology schemas into token-level masks enforced during autoregressive generation.\nUnlike type-constrained code generation, IE presents three distinct challenges: (1)~entity types are inferred from free text rather than declared annotations, (2)~the entity registry is built dynamically during generation, and (3)~constraint targets are context-dependent type assignments rather than fixed grammar productions.\nThe grammar layer (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) guarantees structural validity (well-formed JSON with ontology-vocabulary membership), while the semantic layer (\\S\\ref{sec:semantic}) enforces domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry.\nWe describe the problem formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}).\nFormal analysis of constraint composition is deferred to Section~\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\n\\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}\nGiven an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:output-schema}\n\\resizebox{.88\\columnwidth}{!}{$\\displaystyle\n  y = \\bigl\\{\\texttt{entities}: [\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}],\\; \\texttt{relations}: [\\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j\\}]\\bigr\\}\n$}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j$.\nThe output JSON Schema (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\\to$ tail $\\to$ relation $\\to$ head\\_type $\\to$ tail\\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation.\nThis ordering ensures that the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields are generated, which enables domain and range lookups in sequential masking (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).\n\n\\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}\nUsing the ontology $\\mathcal{O} = (\\mathcal{E}, \\mathcal{R}, \\sigma)$ defined in \\S\\ref{sec:background}, we write $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau^h : \\exists \\tau^t,\\, (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h) = \\{\\tau^t : (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\\tau^h$.\nOur enforcement target is \\emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)$ for every triple.\nThe decoder enforces this sequentially: $\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$ at tail position. This is equivalent to the full pair check and strictly stronger than independent domain/range enforcement, because most relations have non-rectangular type signatures ($\\sigma(r) \\subsetneq \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\times \\operatorname{range}(r)$ for all 7 SciERC and 73/96 DocRED relations; see \\S\\ref{sec:rectangularity}).\\footnote{An independent check ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\wedge \\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r)$) would over-permit type pairs by $1.50\\times$ on SciERC and $1.58\\times$ on DocRED, corresponding to 33.3\\% and 36.8\\% spurious type pairs.}\nOur goal is maximizing $P(y \\mid x)$ subject to all triples being type-valid and $y$ being well-formed JSON conforming to Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Grammar Layer}\n\\label{sec:grammar}\n\nThe grammar layer compiles $\\mathcal{O}$ into a context-free grammar (CFG) enforced at every decoding step via logit masking.\nThis compilation proceeds in two stages.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-to-grammar compilation.}\nWe generate a JSON Schema encoding the structure in Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \\texttt{enum} constraints restricting entity types to $\\mathcal{E}$ and relation types to $\\mathcal{R}$.\nThis schema is compiled into an EBNF grammar via XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, producing a pushdown automaton tracking JSON structure and enum membership.\n\n\\paragraph{Logit masking.}\nAt step $t$, the automaton computes grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \\subseteq V$; all others receive logits $-\\infty$.\nThis simultaneously enforces: (i)~structural validity (well-formed JSON), (ii)~relation vocabulary membership ($r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$), and (iii)~entity type membership ($\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$).\nDomain/range constraints are \\emph{not} enforced, as they depend on entity types within each triple; this gap is filled by the semantic layer.\n\n\\subsection{Semantic Layer}\n\\label{sec:semantic}\n\nThe semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.\n\n\\subsubsection{Entity Registry}\n\\label{sec:entity-registry}\n\nAs the model generates the entity list, the semantic layer builds a registry $\\mathcal{D}: \\text{name} \\to \\text{type}$ mapping each entity to its declared type.\nThis registry is populated incrementally: when the model completes an entry $\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}$, the pair $(n_i, \\tau_i)$ is added to $\\mathcal{D}$.\nThe registry is then consulted during relation generation to look up the declared types of head and tail entities.\n\n\\subsubsection{Position Detection}\n\\label{sec:position-detection}\n\nAt each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\\textsc{HeadType}, \\textsc{TailType}).\n\n\\subsubsection{Dynamic Masking}\n\\label{sec:dynamic-masking}\n\nAt type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \\subseteq V$ restricting generation to type-valid tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{Head type masking.}\nAt a \\textsc{HeadType} position, the processor considers the entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ and the current relation type $r_j$ (if already generated).\nIf $h_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$ and $\\mathcal{D}[h_j] \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only that registered type: $S(t) = \\{\\operatorname{tok}(\\mathcal{D}[h_j])\\}$, ensuring consistency with both the entity list and domain constraints.\nOtherwise, if $r_j$ is known, the mask falls back to all domain-valid types: $S(t) = \\{\\operatorname{tok}(\\tau) : \\tau \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)\\}$.\nIf $r_j$ is unavailable, no semantic mask is applied.\n\n\\paragraph{Tail type masking.}\nAt a \\textsc{TailType} position, the head type $\\tau^h_j$ is already determined from the decoded prefix.\nThe processor computes $\\operatorname{range}(r_j, \\tau^h_j)$, the valid tail types conditioned on the generated head type (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}).\nIf $t_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$, the mask further restricts to the registered type when it belongs to this set.\nThis sequential check ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$) achieves exact pair-based enforcement, strictly tighter than an independent range check $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nEach type name $\\tau \\in \\mathcal{E}$ must be mapped to token IDs for logit masking.\nA naive approach maps $\\tau$ to its first subword token and sets disallowed IDs to $-\\infty$, but this fails in two scenarios:\n(1)~SentencePiece tokenizers produce context-dependent tokenizations where the same string yields different token IDs depending on preceding context (e.g., after a JSON delimiter), and\n(2)~ontologies with prefix-shared type names (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}, \\textsc{Sid}, \\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents) map to the same first token, making single-token disambiguation impossible.\nWe resolve both with \\emph{continuation masking}: at compile time, we scan the full vocabulary to build a prefix map identifying, for each type name, all tokens whose decoded text is a valid prefix.\nAt decode time, the semantic mask allows any token that continues a partially-generated type name, disambiguating over multiple steps.\nThis is tokenizer-agnostic, handling byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral) without tokenizer-specific logic.\n\n\\subsubsection{Grammar-Semantic Interaction}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\n\nThe final mask is the conjunction of $G(t)$ and $S(t)$, permitting only tokens satisfying both layers.\nThis embodies an \\emph{enabling dependency}: semantic lookup requires relation types to be valid members of $\\mathcal{R}$, guaranteed only by the grammar layer; without it, semantic-only constraints reduce DR by only 24\\%.\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$, the system falls back to grammar-only control (\\emph{grammar-first fallback}), preserving structural validity at the cost of a potential type violation.\nFormal analysis of composition, fallback behavior, and constraint tightness is in \\S\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1: Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth constraint layers are active throughout a single generation pass.\nThe entity registry is populated as entities are generated and immediately available for relation-level type checking.\nThis is the simpler and recommended strategy. It achieves strong constraint enforcement with higher relation F1 than the two-pass alternative.\n\n\\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nThe first pass generates only entities under grammar constraints; the extracted entities compile an input-dependent grammar that additionally constrains head and tail names to match declared entities.\nThe second pass generates relations under this tighter grammar plus semantic constraints.\nDetailed comparison is provided in the appendix.\n",

```text
1	% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)
2	
3	At each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \subseteq V$ and $S(t) \subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.
4	The final mask is their conjunction:
5	%
6	\begin{equation}\label{eq:composition}
7	  M_{\text{final}}(t) = M_{\text{grammar}}(t) \;\wedge\; M_{\text{semantic}}(t),
8	\end{equation}
9	%
10	so a token is allowed only if it satisfies \emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.
11	The conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$, reflecting the enabling dependency (\S\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\mathcal{R}$, a property guaranteed only by the grammar layer.
12	
13	\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}
14	When $S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\"{\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.
15	We enforce a \emph{grammar-first fallback}: if no semantically valid token survives grammar filtering, semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.
16	Structural integrity is a hard constraint; a type violation is a soft error detectable by downstream validation.
17	Among type violations, DR and CF differ in severity: a DR violation renders the triple ontologically invalid (the entity type lies outside the relation's domain or range), whereas a CF violation means each triple is individually schema-valid but the same entity receives different type assignments across triples.
18	
19	\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}
20	We define the tightness of relation $r$ as:
21	%
22	\begin{equation}\label{eq:tightness}
23	  \kappa(r) = 1 - \frac{|\mathcal{V}(r)|}{|\mathcal{E}|^2},
24	\end{equation}
25	%
26	where $\mathcal{V}(r)$ is the set of valid type pairs and $\mathcal{E}$ the entity type vocabulary.
27	The mean tightness $\bar{\kappa} = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|}\sum_{r} \kappa(r)$ characterizes ontology restrictiveness.
28	Tighter constraints eliminate more grammar-valid tokens at type-bearing positions, increasing fallback frequency and residual DR.
29	This dual-factor pattern (tightness $\times$ baseline model violation rate) is consistent with the cross-dataset results: for calibrated models (Qwen, Llama), SciERC ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$, 24 valid pairs/relation) achieves 94--99\% DR reduction, while DocRED ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$, 5.68 pairs) achieves 45--90\%; including Mistral (whose high baseline ET shifts the regime), the full range across all three models is 42--99\% (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}; quantitative analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:dual-factor}).
30	This pattern is observed across four model-dataset configurations; additional settings would be needed to establish statistical robustness.
31	
32	\paragraph{Non-Rectangularity and Sequential Masking.}
33	\label{sec:rectangularity}
34	% Data source: artifacts/rebuttal/w2_rectangularity_report.md
35	Sequential masking ($\tau^h \in \operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\tau^t \in \operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h)$) is necessary because real ontology type signatures are non-rectangular: 0/7 SciERC and only 23/96 DocRED relations satisfy $\sigma(r) = \operatorname{dom}(r) \times \operatorname{range}(r)$.
36	Independent enforcement would admit 33--37\% spurious type pairs; on SciERC, 100\% of residual DR under S1 are pairs that pass independent checks but violate $\sigma(r)$.
37	
38	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
39	The composition framework scales from worst-case to deployment settings.
40	Fine-tuning narrows the fallback regime: DR drops from 0.08--10.21\% (zero-shot) to $\leq$\,0.20\% (LoRA), and CF from 2.57--12.02\% to $\leq$\,1.18\%, while R-F1 remains within $\pm$0.6\,pp.
41	This confirms the enabling dependency's practical payoff: under fine-tuning, the grammar layer becomes a no-op (models produce valid vocabulary), and the semantic layer alone reduces residual DR by 39--72\% (\S\ref{sec:composability}).
42	
43	The two-layer design reflects when constraint information becomes available during decoding: vocabulary membership is decidable from the current token (grammar layer), while domain/range requires the decoded prefix (semantic layer, \S\ref{sec:semantic}); forward coreference remains outside single-pass decoding, partially addressed by S2 (\S\ref{sec:s2}).
44

      "content": "% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)\n\nAt each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \\subseteq V$ and $S(t) \\subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.\nThe final mask is their conjunction:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:composition}\n  M_{\\text{final}}(t) = M_{\\text{grammar}}(t) \\;\\wedge\\; M_{\\text{semantic}}(t),\n\\end{equation}\n%\nso a token is allowed only if it satisfies \\emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.\nThe conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$, reflecting the enabling dependency (\\S\\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\\mathcal{R}$, a property guaranteed only by the grammar layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\\\"{\\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.\nWe enforce a \\emph{grammar-first fallback}: if no semantically valid token survives grammar filtering, semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.\nStructural integrity is a hard constraint; a type violation is a soft error detectable by downstream validation.\nAmong type violations, DR and CF differ in severity: a DR violation renders the triple ontologically invalid (the entity type lies outside the relation's domain or range), whereas a CF violation means each triple is individually schema-valid but the same entity receives different type assignments across triples.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}\nWe define the tightness of relation $r$ as:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:tightness}\n  \\kappa(r) = 1 - \\frac{|\\mathcal{V}(r)|}{|\\mathcal{E}|^2},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $\\mathcal{V}(r)$ is the set of valid type pairs and $\\mathcal{E}$ the entity type vocabulary.\nThe mean tightness $\\bar{\\kappa} = \\frac{1}{|\\mathcal{R}|}\\sum_{r} \\kappa(r)$ characterizes ontology restrictiveness.\nTighter constraints eliminate more grammar-valid tokens at type-bearing positions, increasing fallback frequency and residual DR.\nThis dual-factor pattern (tightness $\\times$ baseline model violation rate) is consistent with the cross-dataset results: for calibrated models (Qwen, Llama), SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$, 24 valid pairs/relation) achieves 94--99\\% DR reduction, while DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$, 5.68 pairs) achieves 45--90\\%; including Mistral (whose high baseline ET shifts the regime), the full range across all three models is 42--99\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}; quantitative analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:dual-factor}).\nThis pattern is observed across four model-dataset configurations; additional settings would be needed to establish statistical robustness.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-Rectangularity and Sequential Masking.}\n\\label{sec:rectangularity}\n% Data source: artifacts/rebuttal/w2_rectangularity_report.md\nSequential masking ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$) is necessary because real ontology type signatures are non-rectangular: 0/7 SciERC and only 23/96 DocRED relations satisfy $\\sigma(r) = \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\times \\operatorname{range}(r)$.\nIndependent enforcement would admit 33--37\\% spurious type pairs; on SciERC, 100\\% of residual DR under S1 are pairs that pass independent checks but violate $\\sigma(r)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nThe composition framework scales from worst-case to deployment settings.\nFine-tuning narrows the fallback regime: DR drops from 0.08--10.21\\% (zero-shot) to $\\leq$\\,0.20\\% (LoRA), and CF from 2.57--12.02\\% to $\\leq$\\,1.18\\%, while R-F1 remains within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nThis confirms the enabling dependency's practical payoff: under fine-tuning, the grammar layer becomes a no-op (models produce valid vocabulary), and the semantic layer alone reduces residual DR by 39--72\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:composability}).\n\nThe two-layer design reflects when constraint information becomes available during decoding: vocabulary membership is decidable from the current token (grammar layer), while domain/range requires the decoded prefix (semantic layer, \\S\\ref{sec:semantic}); forward coreference remains outside single-pass decoding, partially addressed by S2 (\\S\\ref{sec:s2}).\n",
      "numLines": 44,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 44
    }
  },

}
