    "originalFile": "% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)\n\nAt each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \\subseteq V$ and $S(t) \\subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.\nThe final mask is their conjunction:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:composition}\n  M_{\\text{final}}(t) = M_{\\text{grammar}}(t) \\;\\wedge\\; M_{\\text{semantic}}(t),\n\\end{equation}\n%\nso a token is allowed only if it satisfies \\emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.\nThe conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$, reflecting the enabling dependency (\\S\\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\\mathcal{R}$, a property guaranteed only by the grammar layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\\\"{\\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.\nWe enforce a \\emph{grammar-first fallback}: if no semantically valid token survives grammar filtering, semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.\nStructural integrity is a hard constraint; a type violation is a soft error detectable by downstream validation.\nAmong type violations, DR and CF differ in severity: a DR violation renders the triple ontologically invalid (the entity type lies outside the relation's domain or range), whereas a CF violation means each triple is individually schema-valid but the same entity receives different type assignments across triples.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}\nWe define the tightness of relation $r$ as:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:tightness}\n  \\kappa(r) = 1 - \\frac{|\\mathcal{V}(r)|}{|\\mathcal{E}|^2},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $\\mathcal{V}(r)$ is the set of valid type pairs and $\\mathcal{E}$ the entity type vocabulary.\nThe mean tightness $\\bar{\\kappa} = \\frac{1}{|\\mathcal{R}|}\\sum_{r} \\kappa(r)$ characterizes ontology restrictiveness.\nTighter constraints eliminate more grammar-valid tokens at type-bearing positions, increasing fallback frequency and residual DR.\nThis dual-factor pattern (tightness $\\times$ baseline violation rate) predicts the cross-dataset results: SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) achieves 94--99\\% DR reduction vs.\\ DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$) at 45--90\\%; the full range across three models is 42--99\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}; Appendix~\\ref{app:dual-factor}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-Rectangularity and Sequential Masking.}\n\\label{sec:rectangularity}\n% Data source: artifacts/rebuttal/w2_rectangularity_report.md\nSequential masking ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$) is necessary because real ontology type signatures are non-rectangular: 0/7 SciERC and only 23/96 DocRED relations satisfy $\\sigma(r) = \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\times \\operatorname{range}(r)$.\nIndependent enforcement would admit 33--37\\% spurious type pairs; on SciERC, 100\\% of residual DR under S1 are pairs that pass independent checks but violate $\\sigma(r)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nThe composition framework scales from worst-case to deployment settings.\nFine-tuning narrows the fallback regime: DR drops from 0.08--10.21\\% (zero-shot) to $\\leq$\\,0.20\\% (LoRA), and CF from 2.57--12.02\\% to $\\leq$\\,1.18\\%, while R-F1 remains within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nThis confirms the enabling dependency's practical payoff: under fine-tuning, the grammar layer becomes a no-op (models produce valid vocabulary), and the semantic layer alone reduces residual DR by 39--72\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:composability}).\n\nThe two-layer design reflects when constraint information becomes available during decoding: vocabulary membership is decidable from the current token (grammar layer), while domain/range requires the decoded prefix (semantic layer, \\S\\ref{sec:semantic}); forward coreference remains outside single-pass decoding, partially addressed by S2 (\\S\\ref{sec:s2}).\n",

   - **D061 (completion)**: Incorporate WikiEvents EE fine-tuning composability results into the paper + retract D059 limitation

   - **P0+P1 Claim Upgrade**: Strengthen CF tradeoff narrative (severity distinction, root cause, EE vs RE CF, 70B CF explanation), upgrade 70B DR=0% prominence, upgrade FT composability contribution
   - **Page Compression**: Compress main body from 10 pages to 8 pages using three strategies (Related Work→Appendix, Violation Landscape compression, Method micro-compression)

   Communication protocol: Paper Agent's plain text is invisible to Director; all reporting done via `paper_agent_post_to_director` MCP tool.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **OntoCoDec**: Two-layer constrained decoding (grammar + semantic) for IE
   - **DR vs CF severity**: DR = hard constraint (triple ontologically invalid), CF = soft preference (each triple individually valid, but entity has different types across triples)
   - **CF root cause**: 100% introduced by semantic layer (grammar-only CF ≈ 0.08%)
   - **EE CF > RE CF**: EE multi-argument event structures increase entity reuse across slots
   - **70B CF baseline**: Low unconstrained CF (0.07%) reflects under-measurement (malformed outputs bypass checker), not genuine consistency
   - **Constraint tightness (κ)**: SciERC κ̄=0.333, DocRED κ̄=0.842
   - **FT composability**: RE: DR≤0.20%, R-F1±0.6pp; EE: RoV→0%, AC≤0.8%
   - **70B DR=0%**: Only configuration to completely eliminate DR on DocRED (998 docs)
   - **EMNLP 2026 ARR**: Long paper limit 8 pages main body (excl. references, appendix, limitations)

3. Files and Code Sections:

     % exp_ids: p1_ee_ft_qwen_wikievents_r5, p1_ee_ft_llama_wikievents_r5
     \paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
     LoRA fine-tuning extends composability to EE (20-document test set).
     Unconstrained FT models retain substantial RoV (Qwen 38.31\%, Llama 8.8\%) and AC violations (2.68\%, 3.5\%); adding S1 eliminates RoV for both models and reduces AC to $\leq$\,0.8\% (Qwen 0\%, Llama 0.8\%).
     Argument F1 remains within $\pm$1.1\,pp (Qwen 7.85$\to$8.18, Llama 9.98$\to$8.91); trigger F1 shows a larger shift ($-$5.4\,pp Qwen, $-$0.6\,pp Llama), though both metrics carry wide confidence intervals at this sample size.
     The violation-elimination pattern replicates RE composability (\S\ref{sec:composability}): FT reduces but does not eliminate schema violations; S1 removes residual RoV and AC.
     ```

     On DocRED (96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$), the 70B model still exhibits substantial violations (RV\,=\,7.81\%, DR\,=\,14.99\%) despite lower rates than 8B models; OntoCoDec achieves \textbf{0\%} across ET, RV, and DR on the full 998-document set with no F1 loss (R-F1 13.07 vs.\ 13.03, $+$0.04\,pp), the only configuration to completely eliminate DR on DocRED.
     CF rises to 7.01\% (from 0.07\%); the low unconstrained baseline reflects under-measurement (malformed outputs bypass the checker) rather than genuine type consistency, while constrained decoding improves output validity and exposes more triples to the consistency check, consistent with the per-slot enforcement trade-off at 8B scale (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).

   - **`artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex`** (Page compression Strategy A)
     - COMPLETE REWRITE: From 27 lines (5 \paragraph blocks) to ~10 lines (single flowing paragraph + appendix pointer)
     - All citations preserved in bundled \cite{} groups
     ```latex
     \section{Related Work}
     \label{sec:related_work}
     
     We provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec.
     Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql,ugare2024syncode,park2024grammar,beurer-kellner2024domino,jsonschemabench2025} guarantees format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints; type-constrained code generation~\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022,scholak2021picard,pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compiles type systems into token-level masks but operates on static type declarations, whereas IE requires a dynamic entity registry built during generation.
     Vocabulary-constrained IE methods~\cite{geng2023gcd,sainz2024gollie,josifoski2022genie,guidex2025,vukovic2024dore,boostcd2025} restrict which tokens can appear but not which type combinations are valid per relation; EnriCo~\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} enforces structural constraints via ASP but requires supervised training and hand-crafted rules; IterGen~\cite{ugare2025itergen} supports semantic constraints via backtracking but requires manual implementation per constraint.
     Training-based methods~\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie,schema_rl2025} provide soft guidance; post-hoc approaches~\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025,slot2025} validate outputs after generation.
     OntoCoDec prevents violations during decoding, is training-free, and compiles constraints automatically from ontology schemas.
     Recent work shows constrained decoding can degrade reasoning~\cite{banerjee2025crane,grammar_aligned_2024,schall2025hiddencost,formattax2026,dccd2026}; however, extraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\cite{tam2024letmespeak}, and OntoCoDec constrains only type tokens while leaving entity spans unconstrained.
     ```

        "content": "% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)\n\nAt each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \\subseteq V$ and $S(t) \\subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.\nThe final mask is their conjunction:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:composition}\n  M_{\\text{final}}(t) = M_{\\text{grammar}}(t) \\;\\wedge\\; M_{\\text{semantic}}(t),\n\\end{equation}\n%\nso a token is allowed only if it satisfies \\emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.\nThe conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$, reflecting the enabling dependency (\\S\\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\\mathcal{R}$, a property guaranteed only by the grammar layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\\\"{\\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.\nWe enforce a \\emph{grammar-first fallback}: if no semantically valid token survives grammar filtering, semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.\nStructural integrity is a hard constraint; a type violation is a soft error detectable by downstream validation.\nAmong type violations, DR and CF differ in severity: a DR violation renders the triple ontologically invalid (the entity type lies outside the relation's domain or range), whereas a CF violation means each triple is individually schema-valid but the same entity receives different type assignments across triples.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}\nWe define the tightness of relation $r$ as:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:tightness}\n  \\kappa(r) = 1 - \\frac{|\\mathcal{V}(r)|}{|\\mathcal{E}|^2},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $\\mathcal{V}(r)$ is the set of valid type pairs and $\\mathcal{E}$ the entity type vocabulary.\nThe mean tightness $\\bar{\\kappa} = \\frac{1}{|\\mathcal{R}|}\\sum_{r} \\kappa(r)$ characterizes ontology restrictiveness.\nTighter constraints eliminate more grammar-valid tokens at type-bearing positions, increasing fallback frequency and residual DR.\nThis dual-factor pattern (tightness $\\times$ baseline violation rate) predicts the cross-dataset results: SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) achieves 94--99\\% DR reduction vs.\\ DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$) at 45--90\\%; the full range across three models is 42--99\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}; Appendix~\\ref{app:dual-factor}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-Rectangularity and Sequential Masking.}\n\\label{sec:rectangularity}\n% Data source: artifacts/rebuttal/w2_rectangularity_report.md\nSequential masking ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$) is necessary because real ontology type signatures are non-rectangular: 0/7 SciERC and only 23/96 DocRED relations satisfy $\\sigma(r) = \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\times \\operatorname{range}(r)$.\nIndependent enforcement would admit 33--37\\% spurious type pairs; on SciERC, 100\\% of residual DR under S1 are pairs that pass independent checks but violate $\\sigma(r)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nFine-tuning narrows the fallback regime: DR drops from 0.08--10.21\\% (zero-shot) to $\\leq$\\,0.20\\% (LoRA), and CF from 2.57--12.02\\% to $\\leq$\\,1.18\\%, while R-F1 remains within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:composability}).\nUnder fine-tuning, the grammar layer becomes a no-op and the semantic layer alone reduces residual DR by 39--72\\%.\n",

        "content": "% Section 3 — Method (Draft)\n% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction\n% via Compiled Ontology Constraints\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe present OntoCoDec, a two-layer constrained decoding framework that compiles ontology schemas into token-level masks enforced during autoregressive generation.\nUnlike type-constrained code generation, IE presents three distinct challenges: (1)~entity types are inferred from free text rather than declared annotations, (2)~the entity registry is built dynamically during generation, and (3)~constraint targets are context-dependent type assignments rather than fixed grammar productions.\nThe grammar layer (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) guarantees structural validity (well-formed JSON with ontology-vocabulary membership), while the semantic layer (\\S\\ref{sec:semantic}) enforces domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry.\nWe describe the problem formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}).\nFormal analysis of constraint composition is deferred to Section~\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\n\\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}\nGiven an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:output-schema}\n\\resizebox{.88\\columnwidth}{!}{$\\displaystyle\n  y = \\bigl\\{\\texttt{entities}: [\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}],\\; \\texttt{relations}: [\\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j\\}]\\bigr\\}\n$}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j$.\nThe output JSON Schema (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\\to$ tail $\\to$ relation $\\to$ head\\_type $\\to$ tail\\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation.\nThis ordering ensures that the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields are generated, which enables domain and range lookups in sequential masking (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).\n\n\\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}\nUsing the ontology $\\mathcal{O} = (\\mathcal{E}, \\mathcal{R}, \\sigma)$ defined in \\S\\ref{sec:background}, we write $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau^h : \\exists \\tau^t,\\, (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h) = \\{\\tau^t : (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\\tau^h$.\nOur enforcement target is \\emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)$ for every triple.\nThe decoder enforces this sequentially: $\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$ at tail position. This is equivalent to the full pair check and strictly stronger than independent domain/range enforcement, because most relations have non-rectangular type signatures ($\\sigma(r) \\subsetneq \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\times \\operatorname{range}(r)$ for all 7 SciERC and 73/96 DocRED relations; see \\S\\ref{sec:rectangularity}).\\footnote{An independent check ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\wedge \\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r)$) would over-permit type pairs by $1.50\\times$ on SciERC and $1.58\\times$ on DocRED, corresponding to 33.3\\% and 36.8\\% spurious type pairs.}\nOur goal is maximizing $P(y \\mid x)$ subject to all triples being type-valid and $y$ being well-formed JSON conforming to Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Grammar Layer}\n\\label{sec:grammar}\n\nThe grammar layer compiles $\\mathcal{O}$ into a context-free grammar (CFG) enforced at every decoding step via logit masking.\nThis compilation proceeds in two stages.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-to-grammar compilation.}\nWe generate a JSON Schema encoding the structure in Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \\texttt{enum} constraints restricting entity types to $\\mathcal{E}$ and relation types to $\\mathcal{R}$.\nThis schema is compiled into an EBNF grammar via XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, producing a pushdown automaton tracking JSON structure and enum membership.\n\n\\paragraph{Logit masking.}\nAt step $t$, the automaton computes grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \\subseteq V$; all others receive logits $-\\infty$.\nThis simultaneously enforces: (i)~structural validity (well-formed JSON), (ii)~relation vocabulary membership ($r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$), and (iii)~entity type membership ($\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$).\nDomain/range constraints are \\emph{not} enforced, as they depend on entity types within each triple; this gap is filled by the semantic layer.\n\n\\subsection{Semantic Layer}\n\\label{sec:semantic}\n\nThe semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.\n\n\\subsubsection{Entity Registry}\n\\label{sec:entity-registry}\n\nAs the model generates the entity list, the semantic layer builds a registry $\\mathcal{D}: \\text{name} \\to \\text{type}$ mapping each entity to its declared type.\nThis registry is populated incrementally: when the model completes an entry $\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}$, the pair $(n_i, \\tau_i)$ is added to $\\mathcal{D}$.\nThe registry is then consulted during relation generation to look up the declared types of head and tail entities.\n\n\\subsubsection{Position Detection}\n\\label{sec:position-detection}\n\nAt each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\\textsc{HeadType}, \\textsc{TailType}).\n\n\\subsubsection{Dynamic Masking}\n\\label{sec:dynamic-masking}\n\nAt type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \\subseteq V$ restricting generation to type-valid tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{Head type masking.}\nAt a \\textsc{HeadType} position, the processor considers the entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ and the current relation type $r_j$ (if already generated).\nIf $h_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$ and $\\mathcal{D}[h_j] \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only that registered type: $S(t) = \\{\\operatorname{tok}(\\mathcal{D}[h_j])\\}$, ensuring consistency with both the entity list and domain constraints.\nOtherwise, if $r_j$ is known, the mask falls back to all domain-valid types: $S(t) = \\{\\operatorname{tok}(\\tau) : \\tau \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)\\}$.\nIf $r_j$ is unavailable, no semantic mask is applied.\n\n\\paragraph{Tail type masking.}\nAt a \\textsc{TailType} position, the head type $\\tau^h_j$ is already determined from the decoded prefix.\nThe processor computes $\\operatorname{range}(r_j, \\tau^h_j)$, the valid tail types conditioned on the generated head type (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}).\nIf $t_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$, the mask further restricts to the registered type when it belongs to this set.\nThis sequential check ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$) achieves exact pair-based enforcement, strictly tighter than an independent range check $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nEach type name $\\tau \\in \\mathcal{E}$ must be mapped to token IDs for logit masking.\nA naive approach maps $\\tau$ to its first subword token and sets disallowed IDs to $-\\infty$, but this fails in two scenarios:\n(1)~SentencePiece tokenizers produce context-dependent tokenizations where the same string yields different token IDs depending on preceding context (e.g., after a JSON delimiter), and\n(2)~ontologies with prefix-shared type names (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}, \\textsc{Sid}, \\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents) map to the same first token, making single-token disambiguation impossible.\nWe resolve both with \\emph{continuation masking}: at compile time, we scan the full vocabulary to build a prefix map identifying, for each type name, all tokens whose decoded text is a valid prefix.\nAt decode time, the semantic mask allows any token that continues a partially-generated type name, disambiguating over multiple steps.\nThis is tokenizer-agnostic, handling byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral) without tokenizer-specific logic.\n\n\\subsubsection{Grammar-Semantic Interaction}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\n\nThe final mask is the conjunction of $G(t)$ and $S(t)$, permitting only tokens satisfying both layers.\nThis embodies an \\emph{enabling dependency}: semantic lookup requires relation types to be valid members of $\\mathcal{R}$, guaranteed only by the grammar layer; without it, semantic-only constraints reduce DR by only 24\\%.\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$, the system falls back to grammar-only control (\\emph{grammar-first fallback}), preserving structural validity at the cost of a potential type violation.\nFormal analysis of composition, fallback behavior, and constraint tightness is in \\S\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1: Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth constraint layers are active throughout a single generation pass.\nThe entity registry is populated as entities are generated and immediately available for relation-level type checking.\nThis is the simpler and recommended strategy. It achieves strong constraint enforcement with higher relation F1 than the two-pass alternative.\n\n\\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nA first pass extracts entities under grammar constraints; extracted entities compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass, which generates relations under both grammar and semantic constraints.\nS1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.\n",

        "content": "% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)\n% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,\n%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema\n\n\\label{sec:violation_landscape}\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.}\nWe evaluate on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$).\nPrimary model: Qwen3-8B~\\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \\S\\ref{sec:composability}.\nWe measure four violation categories: \\textbf{DR} (domain/range type pair $\\notin \\sigma(r)$), \\textbf{RV} (relation $\\notin \\mathcal{R}$), \\textbf{ET} (entity type $\\notin \\mathcal{E}$), and \\textbf{CF} (same entity assigned conflicting types across triples).\nBaseline DR violations are 15.37\\% (SciERC) and 18.46\\% (DocRED), comparable rates driven by constraint tightness rather than ontology scale (\\S\\ref{sec:introduction}); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\\% on SciERC but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\n",
        "numLines": 12,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 12
      }
    },

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/main.tex",
        "content": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{enumitem}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{pifont}\n\n\\title{OntoCoDec: Type-Constrained Information Extraction\\\\via Compiled Ontology Constraints}\n\n\\author{Anonymous ACL submission}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n% === Abstract ===\n\\input{abstract}\n\n% === §1 Introduction ===\n\\input{section1_introduction_draft}\n\n% === §2 Background ===\n\\input{section2_background}\n\n% === §3 Method ===\n\\input{section3_method_draft}\n\n% === §4 Constraint Composition ===\n\\section{Constraint Composition}\n\\label{sec:composition}\n\\input{section4_composition_draft}\n\n% === §5 Experiments ===\n\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\input{section3_violation_landscape}\n\n\\input{section5_scierc_draft}\n\\input{section5_docred_draft}\n\\input{section5_crossmodel_draft}\n\\input{section5_wikievents_draft}\n\\input{section5_runtime}\n\n% === §6 Related Work ===\n\\input{section6_related_work}\n\n% === §7 Discussion and Conclusion ===\n\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\input{section7_conclusion_compressed}\n\n% === Limitations (ARR requirement: standalone section, does not count toward page limit) ===\n\\clearpage\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nCF impact is model-dependent: for well-calibrated models (Qwen, Llama), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.27\\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.\nCF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\\,pp). CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).\nEntity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\\,pp for Qwen vs.\\ $-$0.64\\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\\% vs.\\ 6.8\\%).\nZero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\\% compared to $\\sim$45\\% for fine-tuned systems.\nComposability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp; EE: RoV$\\to$0\\%, AC$\\leq$0.8\\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\\%, Llama 0.46\\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.\nThe continuation masking approach (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}) handles multi-token type names and prefix-shared types on the ontologies tested (6--27 entity types); scalability to ontologies with hundreds of entity types has not been validated, and the prefix-map size may grow substantially.\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated only on 8B-class models.\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern's predictive scope may differ at other scales.\nCF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.\nResidual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\nSchema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) are irreducible by decoding-time constraints alone (training-set frequency coverage); complementary approaches such as global type consensus or post-hoc correction may address this limitation.\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC test set (551 documents) and a DocRED subset across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.\nEvaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA) on two 8B-class models; scaling fine-tuning to 70B-class models, other training paradigms (full fine-tuning, RLHF), and non-English ontologies remains to be explored.\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
        "numLines": 109,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 109
      }
    },

{

  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nWe provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec.\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql,ugare2024syncode,park2024grammar,beurer-kellner2024domino,jsonschemabench2025} guarantees format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints; type-constrained code generation~\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022,scholak2021picard,pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compiles type systems into token-level masks but operates on static type declarations, whereas IE requires a dynamic entity registry built during generation.\nVocabulary-constrained IE methods~\\cite{geng2023gcd,sainz2024gollie,josifoski2022genie,guidex2025,vukovic2024dore,boostcd2025} restrict which tokens can appear but not which type combinations are valid per relation; EnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} enforces structural constraints via ASP but requires supervised training and hand-crafted rules; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} supports semantic constraints via backtracking but requires manual implementation per constraint.\nTraining-based methods~\\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie,schema_rl2025} provide soft guidance; post-hoc approaches~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025,slot2025} validate outputs after generation.\nOntoCoDec prevents violations during decoding, is training-free, and compiles constraints automatically from ontology schemas.\nRecent work shows constrained decoding can degrade reasoning~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,grammar_aligned_2024,schall2025hiddencost,formattax2026,dccd2026}; however, extraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak}, and OntoCoDec constrains only type tokens while leaving entity spans unconstrained.\n",
        "numLines": 10,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 10
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex"
  },

1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	Full S1 overhead varies with document length: $+$13.1\% on SciERC (short documents) and $+$2.43\% on DocRED (long documents) under zero-shot greedy decoding, as the fixed per-token constraint cost is amortized over longer generation sequences (Qwen3-8B, RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell).
6	Under LoRA fine-tuning, overhead is $+$10.0\% on SciERC, with absolute latency 37\% lower than zero-shot due to shorter, more structured outputs.
7	Grammar compile is a one-time 44\,ms cost.
8	Details in Appendix~\ref{app:runtime}.
9	
10	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
11	\label{sec:beam-results}
12	Combined with LoRA fine-tuning, beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all residual violations: both Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B achieve zero violations across all four metrics (ET, RV, DR, CF) on SciERC, with R-F1 of 44.23 and 46.11 respectively.
13	Under zero-shot decoding, beam search resolves greedy's two residual limitations on SciERC (551 documents): DR drops to 0\% for calibrated models (Qwen 0.93\%\,$\to$\,0\%; Llama 0.08\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and CF decreases substantially (Qwen 9.90\%\,$\to$\,0.53\%; Llama 6.56\%\,$\to$\,0.93\%; Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
14	
15	% exp_ids: 70b_scierc_unconstrained, 70b_scierc_constrained, 70b_docred_unconstrained, 70b_docred_constrained
16	\paragraph{Cross-scale generalizability.}
17	\label{sec:70b-results}
18	To evaluate cross-scale generalizability, we test OntoCoDec on Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4 quantization).
19	On SciERC, the 70B model produces fewer baseline violations than its 8B counterpart (DR 6.5\% vs.\ 13.3\%), yet violations persist; OntoCoDec eliminates them entirely (DR$\to$0\%, R-F1 $-$0.03\,pp).
20	On DocRED (96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$), the 70B model still exhibits substantial violations (RV\,=\,7.81\%, DR\,=\,14.99\%) despite lower rates than 8B models; OntoCoDec achieves \textbf{0\%} across ET, RV, and DR on the full 998-document set with no F1 loss (R-F1 13.07 vs.\ 13.03, $+$0.04\,pp), the only configuration to completely eliminate DR on DocRED.
21	CF rises to 7.01\% (from 0.07\%); the low unconstrained baseline reflects under-measurement (malformed outputs bypass the checker) rather than genuine type consistency, while constrained decoding improves output validity and exposes more triples to the consistency check, consistent with the per-slot enforcement trade-off at 8B scale (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
22	The pattern is consistent: model scale reduces but does not eliminate ontology violations, and constraint enforcement remains necessary even at 70B scale.
23	
24	Appendix~\ref{app:case-studies} presents representative case studies illustrating constraint behavior across violation types.
25	
```

8	\label{sec:scierc-results}
9

64	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
65	%  (a) Core result
66	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
67	\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic: DR elimination at zero F1 cost.}
68	Zero-shot decoding stress-tests constraint enforcement at low extraction quality (R-F1 2--8\%).
69	The full two-layer constraint configuration (S1) reduces domain/range
70	violations from 15.37\% to 0.93\%, a 94\% relative reduction,
71	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093
72	while relation F1 moves from 8.23 to 8.36 ($+$0.13\,pp; bootstrap 95\% CI [$-$0.24, $+$0.58]\,pp, $p = 0.58$, 10{,}000 iterations).
73	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836
74	% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824
75	The difference is not statistically significant ($p = 0.58$); constraint enforcement is F1-neutral on this dataset.
76	Post-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains from aggressive violation removal (SciERC: 8.63 vs.\ 8.36; DocRED: 9.61 vs.\ 8.23), but at the cost of discarding up to 48\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\ref{app:posthoc-extended}); OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation.
77	Per-relation DR drops to $\leq$2.82\% on all types, reaching 0\% on four of seven (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
78	However, constraint enforcement elevates CF from 0.08\% to 9.90\% (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
79	
80	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
81	%  (b) Grammar layer
82	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
83	\paragraph{Grammar layer: cost-free structural guarantees.}
84	Grammar-only constraints eliminate RV (1.39\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and ET (0.40\%\,$\to$\,0\%) with no F1 impact, while DR remains unchanged (15.37\%\,$\to$\,14.84\%).
85	This configuration represents the capability ceiling of grammar-based constrained decoding~\cite{geng2023gcd}, which enforces JSON structure and vocabulary membership but cannot reason about cross-field semantic constraints.
86	Grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\%; Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}), confirming that CF is introduced by the semantic layer's per-slot type enforcement, not by grammar constraints.
87	% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823
88	
89	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
90	%  (c) Enabling dependency
91	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
92	\paragraph{Enabling dependency.}
93	Semantic-only reduces DR by only 24\% (11.65\%) and cannot eliminate RV (1.51\%) or ET (0.40\%), which establishes the \emph{enabling dependency}: without grammar-enforced vocabulary, the semantic layer encounters OOV types with no domain/range rules.
94	Adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\% reduction (11.65\%\,$\to$\,0.93\%).
95	
96	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
97	%  (d) S1 vs S2
98	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
99	\paragraph{S1 vs.\ S2.}
100	S2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\% vs.\ 9.90\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.
101	% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644
102	
103	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
104	%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning
105	%  exp_ids: lora_scierc_unconstrained, lora_scierc_full_s1
106	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
107	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
108	\label{sec:composability}
109	Table~\ref{tab:composability} tests composability across zero-shot vs.\ LoRA $\times$ unconstrained vs.\ S1 for Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B.
110	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_full_s1,
111	%          w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,
112	%          llama_scierc_unconstrained, llama_scierc_full_s1,
113	%          w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval
114	
115	Fine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\ref{tab:composability}).
116	Adding S1 eliminates these: Qwen DR 1.95\%\,$\to$\,0\% ($-$0.09\,pp R-F1), Llama DR 1.11\%\,$\to$\,0.09\% ($+$0.02\,pp).
117	On DocRED, the same pattern holds: Qwen DR 0.76\%\,$\to$\,0.01\% ($-$0.30\,pp), Llama DR 0.49\%\,$\to$\,0.20\% ($+$0.51\,pp).
118	% w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.01%, R-F1=40.56; w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.20%, R-F1=44.23
119	
120	Across all settings, fine-tuned + S1 yields DR\,$\leq$\,0.20\% with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp, which supports orthogonal composability.
121	Post-fine-tuning, grammar is a no-op; the semantic layer alone reduces DR by 39--72\% (Appendix~\ref{app:ft-ablation}).
122	CF interference drops from $+$9.82\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}).
123	Beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates residual DR and reduces CF to $<$1\% for both Qwen and Llama (Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
124

DocRED: 9.61 vs.\\ 8.23), but at the cost of discarding up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}); OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation.\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\% on all types, reaching 0\\% on four of seven (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nHowever, constraint enforcement elevates CF from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Grammar layer: cost-free structural guarantees.}\nGrammar-only constraints eliminate RV (1.39\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and ET (0.40\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) with no F1 impact, while DR remains unchanged (15.37\\%\\,$\\to$\\,14.84\\%).\nThis configuration represents the capability ceiling of grammar-based constrained decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, which enforces JSON structure and vocabulary membership but cannot reason about cross-field semantic constraints.\nGrammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}), confirming that CF is introduced by the semantic layer's per-slot type enforcement, not by grammar constraints.\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency.}\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% (11.65\\%) and cannot eliminate RV (1.51\\%) or ET (0.40\\%), which establishes the \\emph{enabling dependency}: without grammar-enforced vocabulary, the semantic layer encounters OOV types with no domain/range rules.\nAdding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\\% vs.\\ 9.90\\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: lora_scierc_unconstrained, lora_scierc_full_s1\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\nTable~\\ref{tab:composability} tests composability across zero-shot vs.\\ LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 for Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B.\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%          llama_scierc_unconstrained, llama_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval\n\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nAdding S1 eliminates these: Qwen DR 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% ($-$0.09\\,pp R-F1), Llama DR 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\% ($+$0.02\\,pp).\nOn DocRED, the same pattern holds: Qwen DR 0.76\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.01\\% ($-$0.30\\,pp), Llama DR 0.49\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.20\\% ($+$0.51\\,pp).\n% w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.01%, R-F1=40.56; w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.20%, R-F1=44.23\n\nAcross all settings, fine-tuned + S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp, which supports orthogonal composability.\nPost-fine-tuning, grammar is a no-op; the semantic layer alone reduces DR by 39--72\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}).\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates residual DR and reduces CF to $<$1\\% for both Qwen and Llama (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{Zero-shot}\n    & Unconstr.\\ & 15.37 & 0.08 & 8.23  & 31.01 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.93 & 9.90 & 8.36  & 30.98 & $\\downarrow$94\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{LoRA}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{Zero-shot}\n    & Unconstr.\\ & 13.34 & 0.24 & 2.68  & 23.56 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.08 & 6.56 & 2.54  & 23.49 & $\\downarrow$99\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{LoRA}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Composability on SciERC (551 docs): zero-shot vs.\\ LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1.\nAdding S1 to LoRA: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.1\\,pp.\nDocRED: Qwen DR $-$99\\%, Llama DR $-$59\\%.\nF1\\,$\\times$100.}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n",

59	\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}
60	Full S1 reduces DR from 18.46\% to 10.21\% ($\downarrow$45\%), eliminates ET ($\to$\,0\%), and near-eliminates RV (29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%).\footnote{The residual 0.03\% RV traces to a rare XGrammar automaton edge case where subword tokens from different enum entries combine into chimeric values (e.g., \texttt{member} + \texttt{icipant\_of} from \texttt{member\_of} and \texttt{participant\_of}). This is not a fundamental limitation of the approach.}
61	R-F1 improves from 7.74 to 8.23 ($+$0.50\,pp; $p < 0.001$), attributable to elimination of 5{,}189 OOV relation predictions.
62	CF increases modestly (0.10\%\,$\to$\,2.57\%), consistent with the CF$\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
63	
64	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_grammar_only
65	\paragraph{Grammar layer surfaces latent violations.}
66	Grammar-only constraints eliminate RV and ET but \emph{increase} DR to 26.17\% ($+$42\%): the baseline's 29.66\% OOV triples cannot be evaluated for DR; grammar enforcement assigns valid relation names whose domain/range rules the associated types frequently violate, \emph{revealing} latent incompatibilities that motivate the semantic layer.
67	
68	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1
69	\paragraph{Enabling dependency and tightness ceiling.}
70	Semantic-only reduces DR by 25\% but cannot eliminate RV (29.87\%) or prevent ET increases (2.48\%\,$\to$\,4.22\%), which replicates SciERC's enabling dependency.
71	Residual DR (10.21\%) exceeds SciERC's (0.93\%) due to higher constraint tightness ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$ vs.\ 0.333): residual DR of 10.21\% implies effective semantic coverage of approximately 90\%, consistent with tight constraints forcing frequent grammar-first fallback (Appendix~\ref{app:fallback-coverage}).
72	Under fine-tuning, this residual narrows to 0.01--0.20\% (Table~\ref{tab:composability}): tightness-driven fallback is largely resolved once model calibration improves.
73

56	\paragraph{Results.}
57	RV and ET are eliminated ($\leq$0.03\%) for all models on both datasets.
58	DR drops 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED; R-F1 improves in five of six settings (DocRED: Qwen $+$0.50\,pp, $p{<}0.001$; Llama $+$0.29\,pp, $p{=}0.013$).
59	Cross-model DR asymmetry reflects baseline RV: Llama's lower RV (16.03\% vs.\ 29.66\%) limits latent violation surfacing, yielding 2.55\% residual DR vs.\ 10.21\%.
60	CF direction depends on baseline calibration: S1 introduces CF for calibrated models (up to 12.02\%; \S\ref{sec:discussion}), while reducing CF for poorly-calibrated Mistral (56.87\%\,$\to$\,1.27\%).
61	Fine-tuning resolves both residual DR and CF: across all four LoRA settings (Qwen, Llama $\times$ SciERC, DocRED), DR reaches $\leq$\,0.20\% and CF\,$\leq$\,1.18\% with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp (\S\ref{sec:composability}).
62

1	% Section 5 — Event Extraction on WikiEvents (compact, inline)
2	% exp_ids: ee_qwen_wikievents_unconstrained, ee_llama_wikievents_unconstrained,

5	% v3 final data confirmed by Director 2026-05-18.
6	
7	\subsection{Cross-Task Validation: Event Extraction}
8	\label{sec:wikievents}
9	
10	To test generality beyond RE, we apply OntoCoDec to event extraction (EE) on WikiEvents~\cite{li2021document} (56 event types, 80 argument roles, 27 entity types, 245 role$\to$type constraints; Appendix~\ref{app:wikievents}).
11	The framework requires no modification: grammar compiles event/role vocabularies into a CFG; the semantic layer enforces role-conditioned type constraints via the same registry.
12	Violations extend to Event Vocabulary (EV), Role Vocabulary (RoV), and Argument Constraint (AC, analogous to DR).
13	
14	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
15	%  Table: WikiEvents three-model comparison (v3 final)
16	%  exp_ids: ee_{qwen,llama,mistral}_wikievents_{unconstrained,grammar_only,constrained_v3}
17	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
18	\begin{table}[t]
19	\centering
20	\small
21	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{2.8pt}
22	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
23	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll rrrr rr@{}}
24	\toprule
25	& & \multicolumn{4}{c}{\textbf{Violation Rates (\%)}}
26	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{F1}} \\
27	\cmidrule(lr){3-6} \cmidrule(l){7-8}
28	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Mode}
29	  & EV\,$\downarrow$ & RoV\,$\downarrow$ & AC & CF
30	  & Trig.\,$\uparrow$ & Ent.\,$\uparrow$ \\
31	\midrule
32	\multirow{3}{*}{Qwen3-8B}
33	  & Unconstr.\ & 24.0 & 74.9 & 0.3 & 0 & 11.8 & 0 \\
34	  & Grammar    & \textbf{0} & 76.1 & 8.2 & --- & --- & --- \\
35	  & Full S1    & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0} & 2.1 & 21.1 & 13.4 & 14.1 \\
36	\midrule
37	\multirow{3}{*}{\shortstack[l]{Llama\\3.1-8B}}
38	  & Unconstr.\ & 10.5 & 88.8 & 0.1 & 0 & 5.7 & 0 \\
39	  & Grammar    & \textbf{0} & 77.0 & 8.8 & --- & --- & --- \\
40	  & Full S1    & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0} & 3.8 & 17.2 & 7.5 & 17.6 \\
41	\midrule
42	\multirow{3}{*}{\shortstack[l]{Mistral\\7B}}
43	  & Unconstr.\ & 37.3 & 63.9 & 0 & 0 & 2.4 & 0 \\
44	  & Grammar    & \textbf{0} & 64.0 & 13.8 & --- & --- & --- \\
45	  & Full S1    & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0} & 1.3 & 14.9 & 3.9 & 11.7 \\
46	\bottomrule
47	\end{tabular}}%
48	\caption{WikiEvents EE results (246 documents, zero-shot).
49	Violation rates over parsed documents.
50	---: not applicable (grammar-only has no semantic-layer metrics).
51	F1 $\times$ 100.}
52	\label{tab:wikievents}
53	\end{table}
54	
55	\paragraph{Results.}
56	Unconstrained generation produces extreme vocabulary violations (EV+RoV exceeding 85\% combined; Table~\ref{tab:wikievents}), and all models yield entity F1\,=\,0 because the parser cannot identify well-formed mentions in off-schema output.
57	Full S1 eliminates EV and RoV across all three models ($\to$\,0\%), which validates tokenizer-agnostic design across byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral).
58	Grammar-only ablation isolates each layer: grammar eliminates EV ($\to$\,0\%) but leaves RoV at 64--77\% because the CFG cannot constrain 80 argument roles conditioned on event context; AC rises from near-zero to 8--14\%, the same latent-violation surfacing pattern observed in DocRED (\S\ref{sec:docred-results}).
59	Full S1 then drives RoV to 0\%, which demonstrates layer complementarity.
60	Residual AC (1.3--3.8\%) traces to confusion between semantically similar entity types (e.g., SID vs.\ SEN) where grammar-first fallback permits the incorrect type.
61	CF rises to 15--21\%, exceeding RE levels (10--12\%) because EE's multi-argument event structures increase entity reuse across slots: a single entity may fill roles in multiple events, each imposing different type constraints through per-slot enforcement.
62	
63	Post-hoc filtering is impractical for EE: it retains only 23/5{,}467 arguments for Qwen (99.6\% discarded), 3/5{,}053 for Llama, and 0/6{,}034 for Mistral, because unconstrained models generate generic role names rather than ontology-specific ones.
64	Unlike RE, where constraints are F1-neutral, EE shows measurable gains: entity F1 rises from 0\% to 11.7--17.6\% and trigger F1 increases by $+$1.5--1.8\,pp, because grammar enforcement produces schema-valid JSON that the parser can process.
65	
66	% exp_ids: p1_ee_ft_qwen_wikievents_r5, p1_ee_ft_llama_wikievents_r5
67	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
68	LoRA fine-tuning extends composability to EE (20-document test set).
69	Unconstrained FT models retain substantial RoV (Qwen 38.31\%, Llama 8.8\%) and AC violations (2.68\%, 3.5\%); adding S1 eliminates RoV for both models and reduces AC to $\leq$\,0.8\% (Qwen 0\%, Llama 0.8\%).
70	Argument F1 remains within $\pm$1.1\,pp (Qwen 7.85$\to$8.18, Llama 9.98$\to$8.91); trigger F1 shows a larger shift ($-$5.4\,pp Qwen, $-$0.6\,pp Llama), though both metrics carry wide confidence intervals at this sample size.
71	The violation-elimination pattern replicates RE composability (\S\ref{sec:composability}): FT reduces but does not eliminate schema violations; S1 removes residual RoV and AC.
72

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, which compiles Information Extraction (IE) ontology schemas into two composable token-level decoding constraints that enforce structural and type-validity constraints during autoregressive generation:
3	(1)~a \emph{grammar layer} (XGrammar context-free grammar) guaranteeing JSON structural validity and entity/relation vocabulary membership (virtually eliminating entity type and relation vocabulary violations); and
4	(2)~a \emph{semantic layer} that enforces per-triple domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry and dynamic logit masking, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
5	%
6	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations: 15.37\% on SciERC (7 relations) and 18.46\% on DocRED (96 relations), with 1--2\% persisting even after fine-tuning; rates are driven by \emph{constraint tightness} rather than ontology or model scale.
7	Composed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning, \textsc{OntoCoDec} reduces DR to $\leq$\,0.20\% with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp of unconstrained baselines (R-F1 40--46): near-lossless composability at deployment-grade extraction quality.
8	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
9	Under zero-shot decoding, which stress-tests constraint robustness at low extraction quality (R-F1 2--8\%), DR drops by 42--99\% across three model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) with no significant F1 cost (DocRED: $+$0.50\,pp, $p{<}0.001$; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}).
10	With beam search ($k{=}4$), the full stack eliminates all four violation types under fine-tuned beam decoding (SciERC, both Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B) while preserving extraction quality (R-F1 44.23 and 46.11, respectively); in zero-shot beam decoding, DR reaches 0\% for calibrated models on both RE datasets.
11	The framework generalizes to event extraction (WikiEvents: 56 event types, 80 roles) without architectural modification and eliminates event-vocabulary and role-vocabulary violations ($\to$ 0\%); where unconstrained outputs are severely off-schema, format compliance additionally enables entity extraction (Entity F1: 0\%\,$\to$\,12--18\%).
12	Fine-tuning composability extends to EE: LoRA + S1 eliminates RoV and reduces AC to $\leq$\,0.8\% on WikiEvents.
13	Per-slot enforcement introduces a cross-field consistency trade-off (up to 12\% on RE and 21\% on EE in zero-shot, resolved to $\leq$1.2\% by fine-tuning on RE).
14	OntoCoDec demonstrates that ontology schemas can be compiled into decoding-time constraints that compose losslessly with standard fine-tuning and generalize across four models spanning three tokenizer families and two model scales (8B to 70B). The framework bridges the gap between flexible generative extraction and the type-safety requirements of downstream knowledge systems.
15	\end{abstract}
16	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NpEGCGRVLmQe3Y7SDWEUAq`</summary>

```text
1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity is guaranteed to carry a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	Violations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 1--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning, driven by \emph{constraint tightness} (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.
9	Constraint enforcement matters most at deployment-grade quality (R-F1 40--46\%): at this level, residual type violations propagate directly into knowledge graphs and decision-support pipelines; reliable type-safety enforcement is needed.
10	
11	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
12	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
13	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} guarantees syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
14	Post-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains, but at the cost of discarding up to 48\% of predictions including true positives; at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\%), it discards the majority of outputs, making it impractical.
15	OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation rather than discarding non-compliant outputs.
16	Training-based approaches~\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} provide only soft guidance that cannot eliminate violations.
17	
18	We propose \textbf{OntoCoDec} (\textbf{Onto}logy-\textbf{Co}mpiled \textbf{Dec}oding), a framework that compiles an IE ontology into two composable layers of decoding-time constraints.
19	Our two-layer architecture reflects the observation that structural violations (malformed output, unknown types) and semantic violations (type-constraint breaches) require fundamentally different enforcement mechanisms.
20	The \emph{grammar layer} enforces JSON well-formedness and vocabulary validity via XGrammar.
21	The \emph{semantic layer} maintains a stateful entity registry during generation: as entities are decoded, the registry records type assignments; when relation arguments are generated, dynamic logit masking restricts output to types compatible with the relation's domain/range specification.
22	The layers compose via bitwise AND with an \emph{enabling dependency}: the grammar layer guarantees vocabulary membership, so the semantic layer can look up domain/range rules.
23	Unlike type-constrained code generation where types are statically declared, IE requires a dynamic entity registry built during generation, with constraint targets that depend on the co-occurring relation.
24	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning, OntoCoDec reduces DR to $\leq$\,0.20\% at $<$0.6\,pp F1 cost (R-F1 40--46); under zero-shot decoding (R-F1 2--8\%), which stress-tests constraint robustness, DR drops by 94\% on SciERC while remaining F1-neutral.
25	
26	Our contributions:
27	\begin{enumerate}
28	    \item The first systematic study of ontological constraint violations in LLM-based IE (spanning per-relation tightness analysis, dual-factor characterization, and post-hoc baseline comparison), violation rates are driven by constraint tightness rather than ontology size, and prompt-level intervention is per-relation unreliable.
29	    \item OntoCoDec, a two-layer compiled constraint framework that is the first to enforce pair-based domain/range type constraints via compiled stateful logit masks during autoregressive IE.
30	    Unlike type-constrained decoding in code generation, where output types are statically determined by the grammar, IE requires stateful cross-field constraints because entity type validity depends on the co-occurring relation within each triple.
31	    \item Near-lossless composability with LoRA fine-tuning across RE and EE (three datasets, two 8B-class model families): DR\,$\leq$\,0.20\% at $<$0.6\,pp F1 cost (R-F1 40--46) on RE; RoV$\to$0\% and AC$\leq$0.8\% on EE. Zero-shot robustness across four models (8B--70B), three tokenizer families, and three datasets: DR $\downarrow$76--99\% on SciERC, $\downarrow$42--90\% on DocRED with significant R-F1 gains (Qwen $p{<}0.001$, Llama $p{=}0.013$); WikiEvents vocabulary violations $\to$\,0\% (Entity F1 0\%\,$\to$\,12--18\%); 70B achieves DR$\to$0\% on both RE datasets.
32	    \item Characterization of the CF$\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off inherent in per-slot enforcement (resolved by fine-tuning to CF\,$\leq$\,1.2\%); beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all four violation types under fine-tuned beam decoding (SciERC, both Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B) and reduces DR to 0\% for calibrated models in zero-shot settings.
33	\end{enumerate}
34	
```

under zero-shot decoding (R-F1 2--8\\%), which stress-tests constraint robustness, DR drops by 94\\% on SciERC while remaining F1-neutral.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item The first systematic study of ontological constraint violations in LLM-based IE (spanning per-relation tightness analysis, dual-factor characterization, and post-hoc baseline comparison), violation rates are driven by constraint tightness rather than ontology size, and prompt-level intervention is per-relation unreliable.\n    \\item OntoCoDec, a two-layer compiled constraint framework that is the first to enforce pair-based domain/range type constraints via compiled stateful logit masks during autoregressive IE.\n    Unlike type-constrained decoding in code generation, where output types are statically determined by the grammar, IE requires stateful cross-field constraints because entity type validity depends on the co-occurring relation within each triple.\n    \\item Near-lossless composability with LoRA fine-tuning across RE and EE (three datasets, two 8B-class model families): DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\% at $<$0.6\\,pp F1 cost (R-F1 40--46) on RE; RoV$\\to$0\\% and AC$\\leq$0.8\\% on EE. Zero-shot robustness across four models (8B--70B), three tokenizer families, and three datasets: DR $\\downarrow$76--99\\% on SciERC, $\\downarrow$42--90\\% on DocRED with significant R-F1 gains (Qwen $p{<}0.001$, Llama $p{=}0.013$); WikiEvents vocabulary violations $\\to$\\,0\\% (Entity F1 0\\%\\,$\\to$\\,12--18\\%); 70B achieves DR$\\to$0\\% on both RE datasets.\n    \\item Characterization of the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off inherent in per-slot enforcement (resolved by fine-tuning to CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\%); beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all four violation types under fine-tuned beam decoding (SciERC, both Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B) and reduces DR to 0\\% for calibrated models in zero-shot settings.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
      "numLines": 34,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 34
    }

1	% Section 2: Background (compressed)
2	
3	\section{Background}
4	\label{sec:background}
5	
6	\paragraph{IE Ontology Structure.}
7	\label{sec:bg:ontology}
8	An IE ontology $\mathcal{O} = (\mathcal{E}, \mathcal{R}, \sigma)$ defines entity types $\mathcal{E}$, relation types $\mathcal{R}$, and a mapping $\sigma: \mathcal{R} \to \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{E} \times \mathcal{E})$ specifying valid (head type, tail type) pairs per relation.
9	A triple $(h, r, t)$ is \emph{type-valid} if $(\mathrm{type}(h), \mathrm{type}(t)) \in \sigma(r)$.
10	We define the \emph{constraint tightness} of $r$ as $1 - |\sigma(r)| / |\mathcal{E}|^2$: tighter constraints leave fewer valid options, increasing violation likelihood.
11	
12	\paragraph{Grammar-Constrained Decoding.}
13	\label{sec:bg:gcd}
14	Grammar-constrained decoding restricts each token $y_t$ to a mask $M_g \in \{0, 1\}^{|V|}$ derived from a target grammar.
15	XGrammar~\citep{dong2025xgrammar} compiles JSON Schemas into pushdown automata for efficient enforcement of structural constraints; Outlines~\citep{willard2023outlines} and SGLang~\citep{zheng2024sglang} offer similar capabilities.
16	However, these methods are limited to context-free constraints and cannot enforce cross-field semantic rules (e.g., ``if $r$ = \textsc{Feature-of}, then $\mathrm{type}(h) \in \{\textsc{Method}, \textsc{OtherScientificTerm}\}$''), which depend on previously generated field values.
17	
18	\paragraph{Problem Statement.}
19	\label{sec:bg:problem}
20	Given $\mathcal{O}$ and an autoregressive LM $\mathcal{M}$, we seek constraints $\mathcal{C}$ ensuring: (a)~\textbf{structural validity}, i.e., well-formed JSON with $\mathcal{E}$/$\mathcal{R}$ membership; and (b)~\textbf{type validity}, i.e., $(\mathrm{type}(h), \mathrm{type}(t)) \in \sigma(r)$ for every triple.
21	Condition (a) is context-free; (b) is context-sensitive but \emph{prefix-decidable}: at the point where a type token is generated, all information needed (relation type, entity registry) is available in the decoded prefix.
22	This requires that each entity type name maps to a unique first subword token (\S\ref{sec:semantic}, Implementation), a design assumption verified for all tested configurations.
23	
```

4	OntoCoDec compiles IE ontology schemas into composable token-level decoding constraints that compose losslessly with standard fine-tuning across both RE and EE: DR reaches $\leq$\,0.20\% with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp on RE (R-F1 40--46), RoV$\to$0\% and AC$\leq$0.8\% on EE, and beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all four RE violation types under fine-tuned decoding for calibrated models (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
5	Under zero-shot decoding, which stress-tests constraint robustness at R-F1 2--8\%, DR drops by 42--99\% across three models $\times$ three datasets with no significant F1 cost; constraint effectiveness holds across the full quality spectrum.
6	CF remains an open challenge for per-slot enforcement; global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct resolution path.
7

```text
38	\subsection{Grammar Layer}
39	\label{sec:grammar}
40	
41	The grammar layer compiles $\mathcal{O}$ into a context-free grammar (CFG) enforced at every decoding step via logit masking.
42	This compilation proceeds in two stages.
43	
44	\paragraph{Schema-to-grammar compilation.}
45	We generate a JSON Schema encoding the structure in Eq.~\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \texttt{enum} constraints restricting entity types to $\mathcal{E}$ and relation types to $\mathcal{R}$.
46	This schema is compiled into an EBNF grammar via XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, producing a pushdown automaton tracking JSON structure and enum membership.
47	
48	\paragraph{Logit masking.}
49	At step $t$, the automaton computes grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \subseteq V$; all others receive logits $-\infty$.
50	This simultaneously enforces: (i)~structural validity (well-formed JSON), (ii)~relation vocabulary membership ($r_j \in \mathcal{R}$), and (iii)~entity type membership ($\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$).
51	Domain/range constraints are \emph{not} enforced, as they depend on entity types within each triple; this gap is filled by the semantic layer.
52	
53	\subsection{Semantic Layer}
54	\label{sec:semantic}
55	
56	The semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.
57	
58	\subsubsection{Entity Registry}
59	\label{sec:entity-registry}
60	
61	As the model generates the entity list, the semantic layer builds a registry $\mathcal{D}: \text{name} \to \text{type}$ mapping each entity to its declared type.
62	This registry is populated incrementally: when the model completes an entry $\{n_i, \tau_i\}$, the pair $(n_i, \tau_i)$ is added to $\mathcal{D}$.
63	The registry is then consulted during relation generation to look up the declared types of head and tail entities.
64	
65	\subsubsection{Position Detection}
66	\label{sec:position-detection}
67	
68	At each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\textsc{HeadType}, \textsc{TailType}).
69	
70	\subsubsection{Dynamic Masking}
71	\label{sec:dynamic-masking}
72	
73	At type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \subseteq V$ restricting generation to type-valid tokens.
74	
75	\paragraph{Head type masking.}
76	At a \textsc{HeadType} position, the processor considers the entity registry $\mathcal{D}$ and the current relation type $r_j$ (if already generated).
77	If $h_j \in \mathcal{D}$ and $\mathcal{D}[h_j] \in \operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only that registered type: $S(t) = \{\operatorname{tok}(\mathcal{D}[h_j])\}$, ensuring consistency with both the entity list and domain constraints.
78	Otherwise, if $r_j$ is known, the mask falls back to all domain-valid types: $S(t) = \{\operatorname{tok}(\tau) : \tau \in \operatorname{dom}(r_j)\}$.
79	If $r_j$ is unavailable, no semantic mask is applied.
80	
81	\paragraph{Tail type masking.}
82	At a \textsc{TailType} position, the head type $\tau^h_j$ is already determined from the decoded prefix.
83	The processor computes $\operatorname{range}(r_j, \tau^h_j)$, the valid tail types conditioned on the generated head type (\S\ref{sec:formulation}).
84	If $t_j \in \mathcal{D}$, the mask further restricts to the registered type when it belongs to this set.
85	This sequential check ($\tau^h \in \operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\tau^t \in \operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h)$) achieves exact pair-based enforcement, strictly tighter than an independent range check $\tau^t \in \operatorname{range}(r)$.
86	
87	\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}
88	Each type name $\tau \in \mathcal{E}$ must be mapped to token IDs for logit masking.
89	A naive approach maps $\tau$ to its first subword token and sets disallowed IDs to $-\infty$, but this fails in two scenarios:
90	(1)~SentencePiece tokenizers produce context-dependent tokenizations where the same string yields different token IDs depending on preceding context (e.g., after a JSON delimiter), and
91	(2)~ontologies with prefix-shared type names (e.g., \textsc{Sen}, \textsc{Sid}, \textsc{Side} in WikiEvents) map to the same first token, making single-token disambiguation impossible.
92	We resolve both with \emph{continuation masking}: at compile time, we scan the full vocabulary to build a prefix map identifying, for each type name, all tokens whose decoded text is a valid prefix.
93	At decode time, the semantic mask allows any token that continues a partially-generated type name, disambiguating over multiple steps.
94	This is tokenizer-agnostic, handling byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral) without tokenizer-specific logic.
95	
96	\subsubsection{Grammar-Semantic Interaction}
97	\label{sec:interaction}
98	
99	The final mask is the conjunction of $G(t)$ and $S(t)$, permitting only tokens satisfying both layers.
100	This embodies an \emph{enabling dependency}: semantic lookup requires relation types to be valid members of $\mathcal{R}$, guaranteed only by the grammar layer; without it, semantic-only constraints reduce DR by only 24\%.
101	When $S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$, the system falls back to grammar-only control (\emph{grammar-first fallback}), preserving structural validity at the cost of a potential type violation.
102	Formal analysis of composition, fallback behavior, and constraint tightness is in \S\ref{sec:composition}.
103	
104	
105	\subsection{Decoding Strategies}
106	\label{sec:strategies}
107	
108	\paragraph{S1: Single-pass decoding.}
109	Both constraint layers are active throughout a single generation pass.
110	The entity registry is populated as entities are generated and immediately available for relation-level type checking.
111	This is the simpler and recommended strategy. It achieves strong constraint enforcement with higher relation F1 than the two-pass alternative.
112	
113	\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\label{sec:s2}
114	A first pass extracts entities under grammar constraints; extracted entities compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass, which generates relations under both grammar and semantic constraints.
115	S1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.
116	
```
