For the next thinking about the two strategies, I'm comparing how the constraint layers operate. Strategy 1 keeps both layers active in a single pass with the entity registry immediately available for type checking, while Strategy 2 uses a two-pass approach where entities are extracted first under grammar constraints, then relations are generated with both constraints applied. S1 performs better on relation F1, making it the preferred configuration.

Now looking at the composition section, I need to compress the non-rectangularity discussion. The key insight is that real ontology type signatures don't form rectangular matrices—most relations in SciERC and DocRED don't satisfy the rectangular property. This means sequential masking is necessary rather than independent enforcement, since independent checks would allow a significant portion of spurious type pairs that shouldn't be valid.

For the fine-tuning composability part, I'm examining how the fallback regime narrows when models are fine-tuned with LoRA. The dropout rates decrease substantially across both datasets while maintaining reasonable performance on the main task.

Now I'm looking at the SciERC section and need to compress the zero-shot diagnostic analysis that's currently taking up quite a bit of space.

The key finding is that the two-layer constraint configuration dramatically reduces domain/range violations from 15.37% down to 0.93%—a 94% relative improvement—but this doesn't meaningfully impact relation F1, which stays essentially flat at 8.36 versus the baseline 8.23. The statistical test confirms this isn't significant. Post-hoc filtering does better in zero-shot settings by being more aggressive with violation removal, though the gains vary across datasets.

I can tighten this considerably: the constraint enforcement is effective at eliminating violations but F1-neutral, and post-hoc filtering offers an alternative path to gains through precision improvements. The grammar-only constraints successfully eliminate relation violations and entity type errors without affecting F1 scores, though general discrepancy rates remain largely unchanged. This suggests the grammar layer provides targeted error reduction for specific violation types. Semantic-only reduces DR by 24% but can't eliminate RV or ET, which shows the enabling dependency—the grammar layer then delivers a further 92% reduction. Now I'm testing how well these constraints compose with fine-tuning across zero-shot and LoRA settings.

Fine-tuning alone gets DR down to 1–2% but leaves violations on tight constraints, while adding the grammar layer eliminates them entirely: Qwen drops from 1.95% to 0% with minimal R-F1 trade-off, and Llama goes from 1.11% to 0.09% with a slight gain. The same pattern repeats on DocRED, where Qwen achieves near-perfect constraint satisfaction and Llama shows strong improvements across both models.

When I look at the combined fine-tuning plus grammar approach, DR stays below 0.20% across all settings with R-F1 staying within ±0.6 percentage points, confirming the two components work independently. The semantic layer alone accounts for 39–72% of the DR reduction after fine-tuning, while counterfactual interference drops dramatically from +9.82 percentage points in zero-shot to +1.18 percentage points after fine-tuning, mostly due to schema ambiguity issues. Beam search with k=4 pushes the results even further, eliminating remaining DR and bringing counterfactual interference below 1% for both models. Looking at the results across both datasets, beam search with k=4 successfully eliminates the remaining constraint violations and brings compositionality interference down below 1%. For SciERC, I've saved about 14 lines total through these compressions. Now moving to DocRED, where the zero-shot diagnostic shows that applying S1 cuts the constraint violation rate nearly in half while completely eliminating entity type errors and reducing relation violations to almost nothing, with a modest but statistically significant improvement in relation F1 score. OV triples (29.66% baseline) become evaluable under grammar enforcement, revealing latent type incompatibilities.

Semantic-only constraints reduce DR by 25% but leave RV and ET unresolved, replicating SciERC's enabling dependency. The residual DR of 10.21% exceeds SciERC's due to tighter constraints (κ̄ = 0.842 vs 0.333), suggesting effective semantic coverage around 90% with frequent grammar-first fallback under fine-tuning.

Now I'm working through the cross-model section...

Table~\ref{tab:cross-model} replicates the full setup with Llama-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B under identical prompts, with Mistral providing a third zero-shot baseline. The composability evaluation uses LoRA, which is only available for Qwen and Llama. For the results, RV and ET are essentially eliminated across all models on both datasets, while DR shows dramatic reductions of 76--99%.

On DocRED specifically, DR drops 42--90%, and R-F1 improves in five of six settings with statistical significance. The cross-model differences in DR stem from baseline RV disparities—Llama's lower RV constrains how much latent violation can surface, leaving 2.55% residual DR compared to 10.21% for other models. Calibration also shapes the CF behavior: S1 introduces CF in well-calibrated models (up to 12.02%), but actually reduces it for poorly-calibrated Mistral. Fine-tuning ultimately resolves both the remaining DR and CF issues. Looking at the performance metrics across different calibration approaches—fine-tuning brings both DR and CF well under control while maintaining R-F1 stability. Now I'm moving to the WikiEvents section to compress the introduction about applying this method to event extraction tasks. Unconstrained extraction yields massive vocabulary violations (over 85%), tanking entity F1 to zero since the parser can't handle off-schema outputs. Full constraint enforcement eliminates these violations across all models, while grammar-only constraints leave role violations at 64–77% and expose latent argument constraints (8–14%), mirroring the DocRED pattern. Residual argument constraint violations (1.3–3.8%) stem from semantically similar entity types, and constraint failures rise to 15–21%, outpacing extraction errors at 10–12%.

Post-hoc filtering proves ineffective—unconstrained models generate so many off-schema role names that less than 0.5% of arguments survive filtering. Despite this, extraction shows real gains: entity F1 jumps from 0% to 12–18%, and trigger F1 improves by 1.5–1.8 percentage points.

For LoRA fine-tuning on the 20-document test set, unconstrained models still accumulate substantial role and argument constraint violations; applying full constraints eliminates role violations for both Qwen and Llama while reducing argument violations. Looking at the runtime overhead analysis across different datasets and model sizes. The S1 component adds varying computational costs depending on document length—minimal impact on longer documents like DocRED but more noticeable on shorter ones like SciERC under greedy decoding. Beam search with $k=4$ combined with LoRA fine-tuning achieves zero constraint violations across all metrics on SciERC for both models, with R-F1 scores of 44.23 and 46.11. Under zero-shot decoding, beam search substantially reduces the remaining violations that greedy decoding couldn't eliminate—domain relevance drops to near zero for calibrated models, and constraint failures decrease significantly across both Qwen and Llama variants.

Now I'm looking at how the approach generalizes to larger model scales, specifically testing on Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct with quantization. Even at the larger 70B scale, constraint enforcement proves essential—the model still produces ontology violations that OntoCoDec eliminates entirely, though the constrained decoding does expose a trade-off where consistency checking becomes more visible (CF rises to 7.01%), revealing that the unconstrained baseline was actually under-measuring violations rather than achieving genuine consistency.

Now I'm working through the paper's structure to trim unnecessary sections: removing the case studies reference since readers can locate the appendix themselves, and condensing the verbose contribution statements in the introduction from multiple lines down to concise single-sentence items. OntoCoDec, a two-layer framework that compiles ontology schemas into stateful logit masks enforcing pair-based domain/range constraints during autoregressive IE, unlike code generation where types are statically declared. The approach achieves near-lossless composability with LoRA fine-tuning across relation and event extraction tasks with minimal F1 degradation, while demonstrating zero-shot robustness across multiple model scales, tokenizer families, and datasets with substantial reductions in constraint violations. I'm also seeing that beam search with k=4 effectively eliminates all four violation types when using fine-tuned decoding, and reduces defect rate to zero for calibrated models in zero-shot settings across SciERC with both model variants.

Now I'm estimating the total space savings at around 48-55 lines, which should bring the paper close to a single page. Let me think about additional structural optimizations—converting the subsubsection headers in the Method section to paragraph formatting could save roughly 4 lines of vertical space since they currently each consume about 2 lines. The current structure uses subsections for the Grammar and Semantic layers, but switching to a more compact paragraph-based layout would be more efficient. I'll go ahead and convert those subsubsections to paragraph headings to reclaim that vertical space, then start making the edits in batches.

    "newString": "The grammar layer compiles $\\mathcal{O}$ into a CFG enforced at every decoding step via logit masking, in two stages.",
    "originalFile": "% Section 3 — Method (Draft)\n% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction\n% via Compiled Ontology Constraints\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe present OntoCoDec, a two-layer constrained decoding framework that compiles ontology schemas into token-level masks enforced during autoregressive generation.\nUnlike type-constrained code generation, IE presents three distinct challenges: (1)~entity types are inferred from free text rather than declared annotations, (2)~the entity registry is built dynamically during generation, and (3)~constraint targets are context-dependent type assignments rather than fixed grammar productions.\nThe grammar layer (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) guarantees structural validity (well-formed JSON with ontology-vocabulary membership), while the semantic layer (\\S\\ref{sec:semantic}) enforces domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry.\nWe describe the problem formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}).\nFormal analysis of constraint composition is deferred to Section~\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\n\\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}\nGiven an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:output-schema}\n\\resizebox{.88\\columnwidth}{!}{$\\displaystyle\n  y = \\bigl\\{\\texttt{entities}: [\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}],\\; \\texttt{relations}: [\\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j\\}]\\bigr\\}\n$}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j$.\nThe output JSON Schema (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\\to$ tail $\\to$ relation $\\to$ head\\_type $\\to$ tail\\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation.\nThis ordering ensures that the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields are generated, which enables domain and range lookups in sequential masking (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).\n\n\\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}\nUsing the ontology $\\mathcal{O} = (\\mathcal{E}, \\mathcal{R}, \\sigma)$ defined in \\S\\ref{sec:background}, we write $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau^h : \\exists \\tau^t,\\, (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h) = \\{\\tau^t : (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\\tau^h$.\nOur enforcement target is \\emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)$ for every triple.\nThe decoder enforces this sequentially: $\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$ at tail position. This is equivalent to the full pair check and strictly stronger than independent domain/range enforcement, because most relations have non-rectangular type signatures ($\\sigma(r) \\subsetneq \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\times \\operatorname{range}(r)$ for all 7 SciERC and 73/96 DocRED relations; see \\S\\ref{sec:rectangularity}).\\footnote{An independent check ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\wedge \\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r)$) would over-permit type pairs by $1.50\\times$ on SciERC and $1.58\\times$ on DocRED, corresponding to 33.3\\% and 36.8\\% spurious type pairs.}\nOur goal is maximizing $P(y \\mid x)$ subject to all triples being type-valid and $y$ being well-formed JSON conforming to Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Grammar Layer}\n\\label{sec:grammar}\n\nThe grammar layer compiles $\\mathcal{O}$ into a context-free grammar (CFG) enforced at every decoding step via logit masking.\nThis compilation proceeds in two stages.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-to-grammar compilation.}\nWe generate a JSON Schema encoding the structure in Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \\texttt{enum} constraints restricting entity types to $\\mathcal{E}$ and relation types to $\\mathcal{R}$.\nThis schema is compiled into an EBNF grammar via XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, producing a pushdown automaton tracking JSON structure and enum membership.\n\n\\paragraph{Logit masking.}\nAt step $t$, the automaton computes grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \\subseteq V$; all others receive logits $-\\infty$.\nThis simultaneously enforces: (i)~structural validity (well-formed JSON), (ii)~relation vocabulary membership ($r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$), and (iii)~entity type membership ($\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$).\nDomain/range constraints are \\emph{not} enforced, as they depend on entity types within each triple; this gap is filled by the semantic layer.\n\n\\subsection{Semantic Layer}\n\\label{sec:semantic}\n\nThe semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.\n\n\\subsubsection{Entity Registry}\n\\label{sec:entity-registry}\n\nAs the model generates the entity list, the semantic layer builds a registry $\\mathcal{D}: \\text{name} \\to \\text{type}$ mapping each entity to its declared type.\nThis registry is populated incrementally: when the model completes an entry $\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}$, the pair $(n_i, \\tau_i)$ is added to $\\mathcal{D}$.\nThe registry is then consulted during relation generation to look up the declared types of head and tail entities.\n\n\\subsubsection{Position Detection}\n\\label{sec:position-detection}\n\nAt each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\\textsc{HeadType}, \\textsc{TailType}).\n\n\\subsubsection{Dynamic Masking}\n\\label{sec:dynamic-masking}\n\nAt type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \\subseteq V$ restricting generation to type-valid tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{Head type masking.}\nAt a \\textsc{HeadType} position, the processor considers the entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ and the current relation type $r_j$ (if already generated).\nIf $h_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$ and $\\mathcal{D}[h_j] \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only that registered type: $S(t) = \\{\\operatorname{tok}(\\mathcal{D}[h_j])\\}$, ensuring consistency with both the entity list and domain constraints.\nOtherwise, if $r_j$ is known, the mask falls back to all domain-valid types: $S(t) = \\{\\operatorname{tok}(\\tau) : \\tau \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)\\}$.\nIf $r_j$ is unavailable, no semantic mask is applied.\n\n\\paragraph{Tail type masking.}\nAt a \\textsc{TailType} position, the head type $\\tau^h_j$ is already determined from the decoded prefix.\nThe processor computes $\\operatorname{range}(r_j, \\tau^h_j)$, the valid tail types conditioned on the generated head type (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}).\nIf $t_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$, the mask further restricts to the registered type when it belongs to this set.\nThis sequential check ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$) achieves exact pair-based enforcement, strictly tighter than an independent range check $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nEach type name $\\tau \\in \\mathcal{E}$ must be mapped to token IDs for logit masking.\nA naive approach maps $\\tau$ to its first subword token and sets disallowed IDs to $-\\infty$, but this fails in two scenarios:\n(1)~SentencePiece tokenizers produce context-dependent tokenizations where the same string yields different token IDs depending on preceding context (e.g., after a JSON delimiter), and\n(2)~ontologies with prefix-shared type names (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}, \\textsc{Sid}, \\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents) map to the same first token, making single-token disambiguation impossible.\nWe resolve both with \\emph{continuation masking}: at compile time, we scan the full vocabulary to build a prefix map identifying, for each type name, all tokens whose decoded text is a valid prefix.\nAt decode time, the semantic mask allows any token that continues a partially-generated type name, disambiguating over multiple steps.\nThis is tokenizer-agnostic, handling byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral) without tokenizer-specific logic.\n\n\\subsubsection{Grammar-Semantic Interaction}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\n\nThe final mask is the conjunction of $G(t)$ and $S(t)$, permitting only tokens satisfying both layers.\nThis embodies an \\emph{enabling dependency}: semantic lookup requires relation types to be valid members of $\\mathcal{R}$, guaranteed only by the grammar layer; without it, semantic-only constraints reduce DR by only 24\\%.\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$, the system falls back to grammar-only control (\\emph{grammar-first fallback}), preserving structural validity at the cost of a potential type violation.\nFormal analysis of composition, fallback behavior, and constraint tightness is in \\S\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1: Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth constraint layers are active throughout a single generation pass.\nThe entity registry is populated as entities are generated and immediately available for relation-level type checking.\nThis is the simpler and recommended strategy. It achieves strong constraint enforcement with higher relation F1 than the two-pass alternative.\n\n\\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nA first pass extracts entities under grammar constraints; extracted entities compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass, which generates relations under both grammar and semantic constraints.\nS1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.\n",

    "originalFile": "% Section 3 — Method (Draft)\n% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction\n% via Compiled Ontology Constraints\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe present OntoCoDec, a two-layer constrained decoding framework that compiles ontology schemas into token-level masks enforced during autoregressive generation.\nUnlike type-constrained code generation, IE presents three distinct challenges: (1)~entity types are inferred from free text rather than declared annotations, (2)~the entity registry is built dynamically during generation, and (3)~constraint targets are context-dependent type assignments rather than fixed grammar productions.\nThe grammar layer (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) guarantees structural validity (well-formed JSON with ontology-vocabulary membership), while the semantic layer (\\S\\ref{sec:semantic}) enforces domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry.\nWe describe the problem formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}).\nFormal analysis of constraint composition is deferred to Section~\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\n\\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}\nGiven an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:output-schema}\n\\resizebox{.88\\columnwidth}{!}{$\\displaystyle\n  y = \\bigl\\{\\texttt{entities}: [\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}],\\; \\texttt{relations}: [\\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j\\}]\\bigr\\}\n$}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j$.\nThe output JSON Schema (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\\to$ tail $\\to$ relation $\\to$ head\\_type $\\to$ tail\\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation.\nThis ordering ensures that the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields are generated, which enables domain and range lookups in sequential masking (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).\n\n\\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}\nUsing the ontology $\\mathcal{O} = (\\mathcal{E}, \\mathcal{R}, \\sigma)$ defined in \\S\\ref{sec:background}, we write $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau^h : \\exists \\tau^t,\\, (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h) = \\{\\tau^t : (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\\tau^h$.\nOur enforcement target is \\emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)$ for every triple.\nThe decoder enforces this sequentially: $\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$ at tail position. This is equivalent to the full pair check and strictly stronger than independent domain/range enforcement, because most relations have non-rectangular type signatures ($\\sigma(r) \\subsetneq \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\wedge \\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r)$) would over-permit type pairs by $1.50\\times$ on SciERC and $1.58\\times$ on DocRED, corresponding to 33.3\\% and 36.8\\% spurious type pairs.}\nOur goal is maximizing $P(y \\mid x)$ subject to all triples being type-valid and $y$ being well-formed JSON conforming to Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Grammar Layer}\n\\label{sec:grammar}\n\nThe grammar layer compiles $\\mathcal{O}$ into a CFG enforced at every decoding step via logit masking, in two stages.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-to-grammar compilation.}\nWe generate a JSON Schema encoding the structure in Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \\texttt{enum} constraints restricting entity types to $\\mathcal{E}$ and relation types to $\\mathcal{R}$.\nThis schema is compiled into an EBNF grammar via XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, producing a pushdown automaton tracking JSON structure and enum membership.\n\n\\paragraph{Logit masking.}\nAt step $t$, the automaton computes grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \\subseteq V$; all others receive logits $-\\infty$.\nThis simultaneously enforces:     "oldString": "\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nEach type name $\\tau \\in \\mathcal{E}$ must be mapped to token IDs for logit masking.\nA naive approach maps $\\tau$ to its first subword token and sets disallowed IDs to $-\\infty$, but this fails in two scenarios:\n(1)~SentencePiece tokenizers produce context-dependent tokenizations where the same string yields different token IDs depending on preceding context (e.g., after a JSON delimiter), and\n(2)~ontologies with prefix-shared type names (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}, \\textsc{Sid}, \\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents) map to the same first token, making single-token disambiguation impossible.\nWe resolve both with \\emph{continuation masking}: at compile time, we scan the full vocabulary to build a prefix map identifying, for each type name, all tokens whose decoded text is a valid prefix.\nAt decode time, the semantic mask allows any token that continues a partially-generated type name, disambiguating over multiple steps.\nThis is tokenizer-agnostic, handling byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral) without tokenizer-specific logic.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nMapping type names to token IDs is non-trivial: SentencePiece tokenizers produce context-dependent tokenizations, and prefix-shared types (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}, \\textsc{Sid}, \\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents) share first tokens.\nWe use \\emph{continuation masking}: at compile time, a prefix map identifies all tokens whose decoded text is a valid prefix of each type name; at decode time, the mask allows any continuation token, disambiguating over multiple steps.\nThis handles byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral) without tokenizer-specific logic.",
    "originalFile": "% Section 3 — Method (Draft)\n% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction\n% via Compiled Ontology Constraints\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe present OntoCoDec, a two-layer constrained decoding framework that compiles ontology schemas into token-level masks enforced during autoregressive generation.\nUnlike type-constrained code generation, IE presents three distinct challenges: (1)~entity types are inferred from free text rather than declared annotations, (2)~the entity registry is built dynamically during generation, and (3)~constraint targets are context-dependent type assignments rather than fixed grammar productions.\nThe grammar layer (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) guarantees structural validity (well-formed JSON with ontology-vocabulary membership), while the semantic layer (\\S\\ref{sec:semantic}) enforces domain/range type constraints via a stateful entity registry.\nWe describe the problem formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}).\nFormal analysis of constraint composition is deferred to Section~\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\n\\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}\nGiven an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:output-schema}\n\\resizebox{.88\\columnwidth}{!}{$\\displaystyle\n  y = \\bigl\\{\\texttt{entities}: [\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}],\\; \\texttt{relations}: [\\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j\\}]\\bigr\\}\n$}\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\\tau^h_j, \\tau^t_j$.\nThe output JSON Schema (\\S\\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\\to$ tail $\\to$ relation $\\to$ head\\_type $\\to$ tail\\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation.\nThis ordering ensures that the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields are generated, which enables domain and range lookups in sequential masking (\\S\\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).\n\n\\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}\nUsing the ontology $\\mathcal{O} = (\\mathcal{E}, \\mathcal{R}, \\sigma)$ defined in \\S\\ref{sec:background}, we write $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau^h : \\exists \\tau^t,\\, (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h) = \\{\\tau^t : (\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)\\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\\tau^h$.\nOur enforcement target is \\emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\\tau^h, \\tau^t) \\in \\sigma(r)$ for every triple.\nThe decoder enforces this sequentially: $\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$ at tail position. This is equivalent to the full pair check and strictly stronger than independent domain/range enforcement, because most relations have non-rectangular type signatures ($\\sigma(r) \\subsetneq \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\times \\operatorname{range}(r)$ for all 7 SciERC and 73/96 DocRED relations; see \\S\\ref{sec:rectangularity}).\\footnote{An independent check ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\wedge \\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r)$) would over-permit type pairs by $1.50\\times$ on SciERC and $1.58\\times$ on DocRED, corresponding to 33.3\\% and 36.8\\% spurious type pairs.}\nOur goal is maximizing $P(y \\mid x)$ subject to all triples being type-valid and $y$ being well-formed JSON conforming to Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Grammar Layer}\n\\label{sec:grammar}\n\nThe grammar layer compiles $\\mathcal{O}$ into a CFG enforced at every decoding step via logit masking, in two stages.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-to-grammar compilation.}\nWe generate a JSON Schema encoding the structure in Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \\texttt{enum} constraints restricting entity types to $\\mathcal{E}$ and relation types to $\\mathcal{R}$.\nThis schema is compiled into an EBNF grammar via XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, producing a pushdown automaton tracking JSON structure and enum membership.\n\n\\paragraph{Logit masking.}\nAt step $t$, the automaton computes grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \\subseteq V$; all others receive logits $-\\infty$.\nThis simultaneously enforces: (i)~structural validity (well-formed JSON), (ii)~relation vocabulary membership ($r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$), and (iii)~entity type membership ($\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$).\nDomain/range constraints are \\emph{not} enforced, as they depend on entity types within each triple; this gap is filled by the semantic layer.\n\n\\subsection{Semantic Layer}\n\\label{sec:semantic}\n\nThe semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Entity registry.}\n\\label{sec:entity-registry}\nThe semantic layer builds a registry $\\mathcal{D}: \\text{name} \\to \\text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}$ adds $(n_i, \\tau_i)$ to $\\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.\nAt each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\\textsc{HeadType}, \\textsc{TailType}).\n\\label{sec:position-detection}\n\n\\paragraph{Dynamic masking.}\n\\label{sec:dynamic-masking}\n\nAt type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \\subseteq V$ restricting generation to type-valid tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{Head type masking.}\nAt a \\textsc{HeadType} position, the processor considers the entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ and the current relation type $r_j$ (if already generated).\nIf $h_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$ and $\\mathcal{D}[h_j] \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only that registered type: $S(t) = \\{\\operatorname{tok}(\\mathcal{D}[h_j])\\}$, ensuring consistency with both the entity list and domain constraints.\nOtherwise, if $r_j$ is known, the mask falls back to all domain-valid types: $S(t) = \\{\\operatorname{tok}(\\tau) : \\tau \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)\\}$.\nIf $r_j$ is unavailable, no semantic mask is applied.\n\n\\paragraph{Tail type masking.}\nAt a \\textsc{TailType} position, the head type $\\tau^h_j$ is already determined from the decoded prefix.\nThe processor computes $\\operatorname{range}(r_j, \\tau^h_j)$, the valid tail types conditioned on the generated head type (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}).\nIf $t_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$, the mask further restricts to the registered type when it belongs to this set.\nThis sequential check ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$) achieves exact pair-based enforcement, strictly tighter than an independent range check $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nEach type name $\\tau \\in \\mathcal{E}$ must be mapped to token IDs for logit masking.\nA naive approach maps $\\tau$ to its first subword token and sets disallowed IDs to $-\\infty$, but this fails in two scenarios:\n(1)~SentencePiece tokenizers produce context-dependent tokenizations where the same string yields different token IDs depending on preceding context (e.g., after a JSON delimiter), and\n(2)~ontologies with prefix-shared type names (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}, \\textsc{Sid}, \\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents) map to the same first token, making single-token disambiguation impossible.\nWe resolve both with \\emph{continuation masking}: at compile time, we scan the full vocabulary to build a prefix map identifying, for each type name, all tokens whose decoded text is a valid prefix.\nAt decode time, the semantic mask allows any token that continues a partially-generated type name, disambiguating over multiple steps.\nThis is tokenizer-agnostic, handling byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral) without tokenizer-specific logic.\n\n\\subsubsection{Grammar-Semantic Interaction}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\n\nThe final mask is the conjunction of $G(t)$ and $S(t)$, permitting only tokens satisfying both layers.\nThis embodies an \\emph{enabling dependency}: semantic lookup requires relation types to be valid members of $\\mathcal{R}$, guaranteed only by the grammar layer; without it, semantic-only constraints reduce DR by only 24\\%.\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$, the system falls back to grammar-only control (\\emph{grammar-first fallback}), preserving structural validity at the cost of a potential type violation.\nFormal analysis of composition, fallback behavior, and constraint tightness is in \\S\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1: Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth constraint layers are active throughout a single generation pass.\nThe entity registry is populated as entities are generated and immediately available for relation-level type checking.\nThis is the simpler and recommended strategy. It achieves strong constraint enforcement with higher relation F1 than the two-pass alternative.\n\n\\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nA first pass extracts entities under grammar constraints; extracted entities compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass, which generates relations under both grammar and semantic constraints.\nS1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.\n",

stages.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-to-grammar compilation.}\nWe generate a JSON Schema encoding the structure in Eq.~\\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \\texttt{enum} constraints restricting entity types to $\\mathcal{E}$ and relation types to $\\mathcal{R}$.\nThis schema is compiled into an EBNF grammar via XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, producing a pushdown automaton tracking JSON structure and enum membership.\n\n\\paragraph{Logit masking.}\nAt step $t$, the automaton computes grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \\subseteq V$; all others receive logits $-\\infty$.\nThis simultaneously enforces: (i)~structural validity (well-formed JSON), (ii)~relation vocabulary membership ($r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$), and (iii)~entity type membership ($\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$).\nDomain/range constraints are \\emph{not} enforced, as they depend on entity types within each triple; this gap is filled by the semantic layer.\n\n\\subsection{Semantic Layer}\n\\label{sec:semantic}\n\nThe semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Entity registry.}\n\\label{sec:entity-registry}\nThe semantic layer builds a registry $\\mathcal{D}: \\text{name} \\to \\text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}$ adds $(n_i, \\tau_i)$ to $\\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.\nAt each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\\textsc{HeadType}, \\textsc{TailType}).\n\\label{sec:position-detection}\n\n\\paragraph{Dynamic masking.}\n\\label{sec:dynamic-masking}\n\nAt type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \\subseteq V$ restricting generation to type-valid tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{Head type masking.}\nAt a \\textsc{HeadType} position, the processor considers the entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ and the current relation type $r_j$ (if already generated).\nIf $h_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$ and $\\mathcal{D}[h_j] \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only that registered type: $S(t) = \\{\\operatorname{tok}(\\mathcal{D}[h_j])\\}$, ensuring consistency with both the entity list and domain constraints.\nOtherwise, if $r_j$ is known, the mask falls back to all domain-valid types: $S(t) = \\{\\operatorname{tok}(\\tau) : \\tau \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)\\}$.\nIf $r_j$ is unavailable, no semantic mask is applied.\n\n\\paragraph{Tail type masking.}\nAt a \\textsc{TailType} position, the head type $\\tau^h_j$ is already determined from the decoded prefix.\nThe processor computes $\\operatorname{range}(r_j, \\tau^h_j)$, the valid tail types conditioned on the generated head type (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}).\nIf $t_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$, the mask further restricts to the registered type when it belongs to this set.\nThis sequential check ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$) achieves exact pair-based enforcement, strictly tighter than an independent range check $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nMapping type names to token IDs is non-trivial: SentencePiece tokenizers produce context-dependent tokenizations, and prefix-shared types (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}, \\textsc{Sid}, \\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents) share first tokens.\nWe use \\emph{continuation masking}: at compile time, a prefix map identifies all tokens whose decoded text is a valid prefix of each type name; at decode time, the mask allows any continuation token, disambiguating over multiple steps.\nThis handles byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral) without tokenizer-specific logic.\n\n\\subsubsection{Grammar-Semantic Interaction}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\n\nThe final mask is the conjunction of $G(t)$ and $S(t)$, permitting only tokens satisfying both layers.\nThis embodies an \\emph{enabling dependency}: semantic lookup requires relation types to be valid members of $\\mathcal{R}$, guaranteed only by the grammar layer; without it, semantic-only constraints reduce DR by only 24\\%.\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$, the system falls back to grammar-only control (\\emph{grammar-first fallback}), preserving structural validity at the cost of a potential type violation.\nFormal analysis of composition, fallback behavior, and constraint tightness is in \\S\\ref{sec:composition}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1: Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth constraint layers are active throughout a single generation pass.\nThe entity registry is populated as entities are generated and immediately available for relation-level type checking.\nThis is the simpler and recommended strategy. It achieves strong constraint enforcement with higher relation F1 than the two-pass alternative.\n\n\\paragraph{S2: Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nA first pass extracts entities under grammar constraints; extracted entities compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass, which generates relations under both grammar and semantic constraints.\nS1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.\n",

    "originalFile": "% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)\n\nAt each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \\subseteq V$ and $S(t) \\subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.\nThe final mask is their conjunction:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:composition}\n  M_{\\text{final}}(t) = M_{\\text{grammar}}(t) \\;\\wedge\\; M_{\\text{semantic}}(t),\n\\end{equation}\n%\nso a token is allowed only if it satisfies \\emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.\nThe conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$, reflecting the enabling dependency (\\S\\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\\mathcal{R}$, a property guaranteed only by the grammar layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}\nWhen $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\\\"{\\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.\nWe enforce a \\emph{grammar-first fallback}: if no semantically valid token survives grammar filtering, semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.\nStructural integrity is a hard constraint; a type violation is a soft error detectable by downstream validation.\nAmong type violations, DR and CF differ in severity: a DR violation renders the triple ontologically invalid (the entity type lies outside the relation's domain or range), whereas a CF violation means each triple is individually schema-valid but the same entity receives different type assignments across triples.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}\nWe define the tightness of relation $r$ as:\n%\n\\begin{equation}\\label{eq:tightness}\n  \\kappa(r) = 1 - \\frac{|\\mathcal{V}(r)|}{|\\mathcal{E}|^2},\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $\\mathcal{V}(r)$ is the set of valid type pairs and $\\mathcal{E}$ the entity type vocabulary.\nThe mean tightness $\\bar{\\kappa} = \\frac{1}{|\\mathcal{R}|}\\sum_{r} \\kappa(r)$ characterizes ontology restrictiveness.\nTighter constraints eliminate more grammar-valid tokens at type-bearing positions, increasing fallback frequency and residual DR.\nThis dual-factor pattern (tightness $\\times$ baseline violation rate) predicts the cross-dataset results: SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) achieves 94--99\\% DR reduction vs.\\ DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$) at 45--90\\%; the full range across three models is 42--99\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}; Appendix~\\ref{app:dual-factor}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-Rectangularity and Sequential Masking.}\n\\label{sec:rectangularity}\n% Data source: artifacts/rebuttal/w2_rectangularity_report.md\nSequential masking ($\\tau^h \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r)$, then $\\tau^t \\in \\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)$) is necessary because real ontology type signatures are non-rectangular: 0/7 SciERC and only 23/96 DocRED relations satisfy $\\sigma(r) = \\operatorname{dom}(r) \\times \\operatorname{range}(r)$.\nIndependent enforcement would admit 33--37\\% spurious type pairs; on SciERC, 100\\% of residual DR under S1 are pairs that pass independent checks but violate $\\sigma(r)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\nFine-tuning narrows the fallback regime: DR drops from 0.08--10.21\\% (zero-shot) to $\\leq$\\,0.20\\% (LoRA), and CF from 2.57--12.02\\% to $\\leq$\\,1.18\\%, while R-F1 remains within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:composability}).\nUnder fine-tuning, the grammar layer becomes a no-op and the semantic layer alone reduces residual DR by 39--72\\%.\n",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section5_scierc_draft.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic: DR elimination at zero F1 cost.}\nZero-shot decoding stress-tests constraint enforcement at low extraction quality (R-F1 2--8\\%).\nThe full two-layer constraint configuration (S1) reduces domain/range\nviolations from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\%, a 94\\% relative reduction,\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\nwhile relation F1 moves from 8.23 to 8.36 ($+$0.13\\,pp; bootstrap 95\\% CI [$-$0.24, $+$0.58]\\,pp, $p = 0.58$, 10{,}000 iterations).\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nThe difference is not statistically significant ($p = 0.58$); constraint enforcement is F1-neutral on this dataset.\nPost-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains from aggressive violation removal (SciERC: 8.63 vs.\\ 8.36; DocRED: 9.61 vs.\\ 8.23), but at the cost of discarding up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}); OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation.\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\% on all types, reaching 0\\% on four of seven (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nHowever, constraint enforcement elevates CF from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nFull S1 reduces DR from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\\,$\\to$\\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\%, reaching 0\\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nPost-hoc filtering achieves slightly higher F1 via precision gains but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\nCF rises from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion})."
}

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic: DR elimination at zero F1 cost.}\nZero-shot decoding stress-tests constraint enforcement at low extraction quality (R-F1 2--8\\%).\nThe full two-layer constraint configuration (S1) reduces domain/range\nviolations from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\%, a 94\\% relative reduction,\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\nwhile relation F1 moves from 8.23 to 8.36 ($+$0.13\\,pp; bootstrap 95\\% CI [$-$0.24, $+$0.58]\\,pp, $p = 0.58$, 10{,}000 iterations).\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nThe difference is not statistically significant ($p = 0.58$); constraint enforcement is F1-neutral on this dataset.\nPost-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains from aggressive violation removal (SciERC: 8.63 vs.\\ 8.36; DocRED: 9.61 vs.\\ 8.23), but at the cost of discarding up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}); OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation.\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\% on all types, reaching 0\\% on four of seven (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nHowever, constraint enforcement elevates CF from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nFull S1 reduces DR from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\\,$\\to$\\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\%, reaching 0\\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nPost-hoc filtering achieves slightly higher F1 via precision gains but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\nCF rises from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",
    "originalFile": "% Section 5 — SciERC Results (Draft)\n% Data source: Phase 2 SciERC registry experiments\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_grammar_only,\n%          phase2_scierc_semantic_only, phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_scierc_full_s2\n% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.\n\n\\subsection{Ablation and Composability with Fine-Tuning}\n\\label{sec:scierc-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} presents the five-way ablation on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 551 documents); only the active constraint layers differ across rows.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: SciERC five-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{5}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-6} \\cmidrule(l){7-8}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 15.37 & ---\n  & 1.39  & 0.40\n  & 0.08\n  & 8.23  & 31.01 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 14.84 & $\\downarrow$3\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.08\n  & 8.23  & 30.98 \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 11.65 & $\\downarrow$24\\%\n  & 1.51  & 0.40\n  & 2.71\n  & 8.15  & 31.01 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{0.93} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{94\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 9.90\n  & \\textbf{8.36} & 30.98 \\\\\nFull (S2)\n  & 0.97  & $\\downarrow$94\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 8.03\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{31.90} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ unconstrained.\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded).\nF1 $\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic: DR elimination at zero F1 cost.}\nZero-shot decoding stress-tests constraint enforcement at low extraction quality (R-F1 2--8\\%).\nThe full two-layer constraint configuration (S1) reduces domain/range\nviolations from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\%, a 94\\% relative reduction,\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\nwhile relation F1 moves from 8.23 to 8.36 ($+$0.13\\,pp; bootstrap 95\\% CI [$-$0.24, $+$0.58]\\,pp, $p = 0.58$, 10{,}000 iterations).\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nThe difference is not statistically significant ($p = 0.58$); constraint enforcement is F1-neutral on this dataset.\nPost-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains from aggressive violation removal (SciERC: 8.63 vs.\\ 8.36; DocRED: 9.61 vs.\\ 8.23), but at the cost of discarding up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}); OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation.\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\% on all types, reaching 0\\% on four of seven (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nHowever, constraint enforcement elevates CF from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Grammar layer: cost-free structural guarantees.}\nGrammar-only constraints eliminate RV (1.39\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and ET (0.40\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) with no F1 impact, while DR remains unchanged (15.37\\%\\,$\\to$\\,14.84\\%).\nThis configuration represents the capability ceiling of grammar-based constrained decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, which enforces JSON structure and vocabulary membership but cannot reason about cross-field semantic constraints.\nGrammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}), confirming that CF is introduced by the semantic layer's per-slot type enforcement, not by grammar constraints.\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency.}\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% (11.65\\%) and cannot eliminate RV (1.51\\%) or ET (0.40\\%), which establishes the \\emph{enabling dependency}: without grammar-enforced vocabulary, the semantic layer encounters OOV types with no domain/range rules.\nAdding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\\% vs.\\ 9.90\\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: lora_scierc_unconstrained, lora_scierc_full_s1\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\nTable~\\ref{tab:composability} tests composability across zero-shot vs.\\ LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 for Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B.\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%          llama_scierc_unconstrained, llama_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval\n\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nAdding S1 eliminates these: Qwen DR 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% ($-$0.09\\,pp R-F1), Llama DR 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\% ($+$0.02\\,pp).\nOn DocRED, the same pattern holds: Qwen DR 0.76\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.01\\% ($-$0.30\\,pp), Llama DR 0.49\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.20\\% ($+$0.51\\,pp).\n% w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.01%, R-F1=40.56; w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.20%, R-F1=44.23\n\nAcross all settings, fine-tuned + S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp, which supports orthogonal composability.\nPost-fine-tuning, grammar is a no-op; the semantic layer alone reduces DR by 39--72\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}).\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates residual DR and reduces CF to $<$1\\% for both Qwen and Llama (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{Zero-shot}\n    & Unconstr.\\ & 15.37 & 0.08 & 8.23  & 31.01 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.93 & 9.90 & 8.36  & 30.98 & $\\downarrow$94\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{LoRA}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{Zero-shot}\n    & Unconstr.\\ & 13.34 & 0.24 & 2.68  & 23.56 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.08 & 6.56 & 2.54  & 23.49 & $\\downarrow$99\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{LoRA}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Composability on SciERC (551 docs): zero-shot vs.\\ LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1.\nAdding S1 to LoRA: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.1\\,pp.\nDocRED: Qwen DR $-$99\\%, Llama DR $-$59\\%.\nF1\\,$\\times$100.}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n",

    "originalFile": "% Section 5 — SciERC Results (Draft)\n% Data source: Phase 2 SciERC registry experiments\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_grammar_only,\n%          phase2_scierc_semantic_only, phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_scierc_full_s2\n% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.\n\n\\subsection{Ablation and Composability with Fine-Tuning}\n\\label{sec:scierc-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} presents the five-way ablation on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 551 documents); only the active constraint layers differ across rows.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: SciERC five-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{5}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-6} \\cmidrule(l){7-8}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 15.37 & ---\n  & 1.39  & 0.40\n  & 0.08\n  & 8.23  & 31.01 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 14.84 & $\\downarrow$3\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.08\n  & 8.23  & 30.98 \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 11.65 & $\\downarrow$24\\%\n  & 1.51  & 0.40\n  & 2.71\n  & 8.15  & 31.01 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{0.93} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{94\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 9.90\n  & \\textbf{8.36} & 30.98 \\\\\nFull (S2)\n  & 0.97  & $\\downarrow$94\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 8.03\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{31.90} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ unconstrained.\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded).\nF1 $\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nFull S1 reduces DR from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\\,$\\to$\\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\%, reaching 0\\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nPost-hoc filtering achieves slightly higher F1 via precision gains but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\nCF rises from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Grammar layer: cost-free structural guarantees.}\nGrammar-only constraints eliminate RV (1.39\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and ET (0.40\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) with no F1 impact, while DR remains unchanged (15.37\\%\\,$\\to$\\,14.84\\%).\nThis configuration represents the capability ceiling of grammar-based constrained decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, which enforces JSON structure and vocabulary membership but cannot reason about cross-field semantic constraints.\nGrammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}), confirming that CF is introduced by the semantic layer's per-slot type enforcement, not by grammar constraints.\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency.}\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% (11.65\\%) and cannot eliminate RV (1.51\\%) or ET (0.40\\%), which establishes the \\emph{enabling dependency}: without grammar-enforced vocabulary, the semantic layer encounters OOV types with no domain/range rules.\nAdding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\\% vs.\\ 9.90\\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: lora_scierc_unconstrained, lora_scierc_full_s1\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\nTable~\\ref{tab:composability} tests composability across zero-shot vs.\\ LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 for Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B.\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%          llama_scierc_unconstrained, llama_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval\n\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nAdding S1 eliminates these: Qwen DR 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% ($-$0.09\\,pp R-F1), Llama DR 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\% ($+$0.02\\,pp).\nOn DocRED, the same pattern holds: Qwen DR 0.76\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.01\\% ($-$0.30\\,pp), Llama DR 0.49\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.20\\% ($+$0.51\\,pp).\n% w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.01%, R-F1=40.56; w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.20%, R-F1=44.23\n\nAcross all settings, fine-tuned + S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp, which supports orthogonal composability.\nPost-fine-tuning, grammar is a no-op; the semantic layer alone reduces DR by 39--72\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}).\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates residual DR and reduces CF to $<$1\\% for both Qwen and Llama (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{Zero-shot}\n    & Unconstr.\\ & 15.37 & 0.08 & 8.23  & 31.01 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.93 & 9.90 & 8.36  & 30.98 & $\\downarrow$94\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{LoRA}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{Zero-shot}\n    & Unconstr.\\ & 13.34 & 0.24 & 2.68  & 23.56 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.08 & 6.56 & 2.54  & 23.49 & $\\downarrow$99\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{LoRA}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Composability on SciERC (551 docs): zero-shot vs.\\ LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1.\nAdding S1 to LoRA: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.1\\,pp.\nDocRED: Qwen DR $-$99\\%, Llama DR $-$59\\%.\nF1\\,$\\times$100.}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n",

    "oldString": "Table~\\ref{tab:composability} tests composability across zero-shot vs.\\ LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 for Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B.\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%          llama_scierc_unconstrained, llama_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval\n\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nAdding S1 eliminates these: Qwen DR 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% ($-$0.09\\,pp R-F1), Llama DR 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\% ($+$0.02\\,pp).\nOn DocRED, the same pattern holds: Qwen DR 0.76\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.01\\% ($-$0.30\\,pp), Llama DR 0.49\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.20\\% ($+$0.51\\,pp).\n% w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.01%, R-F1=40.56; w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.20%, R-F1=44.23\n\nAcross all settings, fine-tuned + S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp, which supports orthogonal composability.\nPost-fine-tuning, grammar is a no-op; the semantic layer alone reduces DR by 39--72\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}).\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates residual DR and reduces CF to $<$1\\% for both Qwen and Llama (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).",
    "newString": "% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%          llama_scierc_unconstrained, llama_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nAdding S1: Qwen DR 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, Llama 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\% on SciERC; Qwen 0.76\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.01\\%, Llama 0.49\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.20\\% on DocRED.\n% w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.01%, R-F1=40.56; w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.20%, R-F1=44.23\nAcross all settings, DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp.\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates residual DR and CF to $<$1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).",
    "originalFile": "% Section 5 — SciERC Results (Draft)\n% Data source: Phase 2 SciERC registry experiments\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_grammar_only,\n%          phase2_scierc_semantic_only, phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_scierc_full_s2\n% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.\n\n\\subsection{Ablation and Composability with Fine-Tuning}\n\\label{sec:scierc-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} presents the five-way ablation on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 551 documents); only the active constraint layers differ across rows.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: SciERC five-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{5}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-6} \\cmidrule(l){7-8}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 15.37 & ---\n  & 1.39  & 0.40\n  & 0.08\n  & 8.23  & 31.01 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 14.84 & $\\downarrow$3\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.08\n  & 8.23  & 30.98 \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 11.65 & $\\downarrow$24\\%\n  & 1.51  & 0.40\n  & 2.71\n  & 8.15  & 31.01 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{0.93} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{94\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 9.90\n  & \\textbf{8.36} & 30.98 \\\\\nFull (S2)\n  & 0.97  & $\\downarrow$94\\%\n  & \\textbf{0} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 8.03\n  & 8.03  & \\textbf{31.90} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ unconstrained.\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded).\nF1 $\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nFull S1 reduces DR from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\\,$\\to$\\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\%, reaching 0\\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nPost-hoc filtering achieves slightly higher F1 via precision gains but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\nCF rises from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Layer contributions.}\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\nGrammar-only eliminates RV and ET with no F1 impact while DR remains at 14.84\\%, the ceiling of grammar-based decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}; grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\\%), confirming CF originates from the semantic layer.\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% and cannot eliminate RV or ET, establishing the \\emph{enabling dependency}; adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\\% vs.\\ 9.90\\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: lora_scierc_unconstrained, lora_scierc_full_s1\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\nTable~\\ref{tab:composability} tests composability across zero-shot vs.\\ LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 for Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B.\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%          llama_scierc_unconstrained, llama_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval\n\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nAdding S1 eliminates these: Qwen DR 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% ($-$0.09\\,pp R-F1), Llama DR 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\% ($+$0.02\\,pp).\nOn DocRED, the same pattern holds: Qwen DR 0.76\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.01\\% ($-$0.30\\,pp), Llama DR 0.49\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.20\\% ($+$0.51\\,pp).\n% w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.01%, R-F1=40.56; w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.20%, R-F1=44.23\n\nAcross all settings, fine-tuned + S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp, which supports orthogonal composability.\nPost-fine-tuning, grammar is a no-op; the semantic layer alone reduces DR by 39--72\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}).\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates residual DR and reduces CF to $<$1\\% for both Qwen and Llama (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{Zero-shot}\n    & Unconstr.\\ & 15.37 & 0.08 & 8.23  & 31.01 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.93 & 9.90 & 8.36  & 30.98 & $\\downarrow$94\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{LoRA}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{Zero-shot}\n    & Unconstr.\\ & 13.34 & 0.24 & 2.68  & 23.56 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.08 & 6.56 & 2.54  & 23.49 & $\\downarrow$99\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{LoRA}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Composability on SciERC (551 docs): zero-shot vs.\\ LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1.\nAdding S1 to LoRA: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.1\\,pp.\nDocRED: Qwen DR $-$99\\%, Llama DR $-$59\\%.\nF1\\,$\\times$100.}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n",
